A.21. Executive summary  (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)  
The agriculture sector in northern Ghana — which supports ~80% of the total workforce — is dominated by smallholder 
farmers practising mostly rainfed, subsistence farming , using mostly traditional agricultural practices and minimum 
technology . 
The long duration of the dry season experienced in northern Ghana results in prolonged periods of food shortages .  
High temperatures in northern Ghana already contribute to reduced water -retention capacity and frequent drying of 
water bodies — such as wells, dams, and streams — that serve as sources of water for both livestock and household 
use, including for dry seaso n gardening.   Increased temperatures linked to climate change exacerbat e the problem 
through the expansion of ranges of the weeds, pests and fungi tha t cause food spoilage .  Con versely, e xtreme rainfall 
events often result in severe flooding across many areas of northern Ghana, destroying crops, taking lives and 
damaging assets and public infrastructures , as well as reduc ing storage capacity of reservoirs in northern Ghana, 
caus ing them to dry -up quicker in the dry season as well as increase the risk of dam collapse, leading to downstream 
flooding.  
 
The proposed project objective is to enhance the climate resilience of vulnerable smallholder farming communities in 
northern Ghana by improving food security and enhancing the agro -based rural economy.  The project strategy is three -
fold comprising the improved climate data and early warnings made available to facilitate proactive drought and flood


---
*Page 9*
---

A 
 GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V. 3.0 | PAGE 3 
 management,  the  adoption of climate -resilient agriculture  and water storage to enable dry season farming, and 
investments in landscape restoration (such as riverbank restoration, agroforestry, reforestation and fire management) 
to improve soil  health and  integrity , physical and land-based water retention and  protect physical assets from flooding.   
 
The project will work at three levels: community level on planning and implementation systems, at the regional level on 
strengthening weather forecast capability that will serve the Northern Sector, complementing investments in forecasting 
capability in th e South of the country  as well as  national level in establishing a national action plan for drought and 
flood hazard management .  The community level integrated ecosystem -based adaptation approach will inform the 
District level planning and budgeting systems, through building capacity and awareness of District planning officers to  
scale up the project approach. C ombined with associated community training, extension services and awareness -
raising, this will  increase opportunities for knowledge and technology exchange between communities, and thereby 
promote autonomous upscaling of these interventions.  
The proposed project will be implemented in the North  East, Upper East and Upper West Regions of northern Ghana , 
in eight districts in northern Ghana that have been specifically chosen because of their high vulnerability to climate 
change impacts .  Across the eight districts, the project will  provide  agricultural and livelihood support for 120 
communities over seven years  as well early warning advisories for the entire population in the 8 Districts .   
The EPA is the main executing entity, that will work in partnership with Ministry of Food Security and Agriculture together 
with the Ghana Meteorological services and Water Resources Commission to deliver an integrated, ecosystem -based 
adaptation strategy that will transition the smallholder farming communities of northern Ghana more climate -resilient, 
productive and sustainable livelihoods.

 PROJECT/PROGRAMME INFORMATION  
B.1. Climate con text (max.  1000 words, approximately  2 pages ) 
Context3 
Approximately 80% of the 4.16 million people living in 
northern Ghana are smallholder farmers depending on 
traditional, small -scale, rainfed agricultural systems to 
generate household incomes and maintain food 
security. Smallholder farming households in northern 
Ghana , comprising of  Upper West Region (UWR), 
Upper East Region (UER), and North  East Region 
(NER), frequently experience severe food insecurity for 
between three to six months annually during the dry 
season.   They a re highly vulnerable to the degradation 
of natural ecosystems and changes in climate .  The 
main drivers of degradation are inter alia: i) 
deforestation for wood and charcoal production and 
clearing for agricultural activities; ii) wildfires to clear 
natural vegetation for agriculture; iii) overgrazing by 
cattle; and iv) soil erosion and fert ility loss because of 
unsustainable farming practice s. 
 
The results of the climate change vulnerability 
assessment (see Section 2 of Annex 2 — Feasibility 
Assessment) demonstrate that th ese administrative 
Regions of northern Ghana , which have high levels of 
poverty,  are the most climate vulnerable in the country  
(Figure 14 – Annex 2 Feasibility Assessment) , and 
therefore, are a priority target for climate change 
adaptation interventions.  
 
Climate analysis4 
 
Current trends  
Most of the rainfall in northern Ghana occurs between 
June and October; this is also the period with the 
largest variation in total monthly precipitation volume . 
Historical trends show a substantial change in rainfall 
patterns  with a reduction in the amount of rainfall during the early wet season (~ -8 mm April, ~ -8 mm May, ~ -17 mm 
June ~ -9 mm July, ~ -12 mm August in the 2010 decade) and an increase during the late wet season (~+5 mm 
September and ~+17 mm October in the 2010 decade). In northern Ghana , rain fall patterns  have  become increasingly 
erratic with the start of the wet season becoming more difficult to predict. In the past, the wet season started in April 
and ended around late September or early October. However, in recent times, the wet season starts in June or July  
with extremely heavy rainfall in September or October, indicative of a shortening of the historical rainfall season. 
Similarly, t he number of rainfall days (days with 0.1 mm or more) has decreased by 5.3 days per year in the peak 
precipi tation months April to September  from the 2000s , with a decreasing trend of 2.6 days per decade.  The overall 
trend has been prolonged dry seasons , an increased frequency and intensity of drought conditions, and a shorter rainy 
season with increased occurrence of extreme rainfall events with severe consequences for smallholder farmers.  
 
Climate change projections  
Temperature :  Under RCP 8.5, the annual average temperature of Upper East Region is predicted to increase from 
28.2 ̊C to 33.1  ̊C (an increase of 4.9  ̊C) by 2085 , higher than the national average of 4.5° C. Annual minimum and 
maximum temperatures are also expected to increase in all the regions of Ghana, with northern Ghana again expected 
to experience the largest changes. The annual maximum temperature of the Upper East Region is expected to increase Figure 1: Ghana ’s 16 Administrative Regions , including the project 
Regions North East, Upper East and Upper West

5 Defined as the amount of evaporation that would occur if a sufficient water source were available.  
6 Annual moisture index = Mean annual rainfall / Potential evapotranspiration.  
7 Please see Section 5 of Annex 2: Feasibility Study for a detailed assessment of climate change impacts in northern Ghana.  
 from 34.1  ̊C to an extreme 38.8  ̊C by 2085 under RCP 8. 5.  In summary, m aximum and minimum temperature will 
increase throughout the year , and h eatwaves will become more frequent.  
 
Potential evapotranspiration :  GCMs suggest that potential evapotranspiration (PET)5 will increase across Ghana 
throughout the 21st century under both RCP scenarios . This increase will be most apparent in northern Ghana where 
PET will increase by 8.5% by 2085. The predicted increase in PET will be marginally greater than the predicted minor 
increase in annual rainfall, resulting in a decrease in the annual moisture in dex6 in most regions of the country.  
 
Rainfall :  Ensemble climate projections suggest that in northern Ghana the change in rainfall to include a reduction in 
early wet season rainfall (April to June) and increase in late wet season rainfall (July to September). The projections , 
under RCP 8.5 scenario  estimate an increase of 5mm/month in April and July/August, increasing per decade towards 
the end of the century. By contrast, the wet season onset and cessation months of March and October are estimated 
to experience a decrease in rainfall per decade of 10mm/month.  For both RCP 4.5 and RCP 8.5, there will be a reduced 
occurrence of low -volume rainfall (<11mm) events and an increased occurrence of high -volume rainfall (11 –21mm) 
events.   In summary, t he rainfall profile in northern Ghana will change  where the wet se ason will be shorter and rain 
will be more likely to occur less frequent ly but more intense ly during high  volume events.  
 
Extreme events :  Extreme rainfall events are likely to become more frequent over  time, with events that historically 
occurred 1 in every ~100 years predicted to occur 1 in ~73 years and 1 in every ~80 years under RCP 4.5 and RCP 
8.5 scenarios, respectively. Furthermore, extreme rainfall events are predicted to become more intense over  time, with 
1 in 10 -year events projected to be ~8.3% more intense (under both RCP 4.5 and RCP 8.5 scenarios) .  
 
The increased occurrence of higher rainfall events is associated with an increase in flood risk, while the overall reduction 
in the number of rainfall events is associated with more frequent dry spells. T he number of dry days per month is 
predicted to increase above the historical average throughout the year under both the RCP 4.5 and RCP 8.5 scenarios, 
with the largest increases occurring in the dry season months of November to February. Noteworthy is the  increased 
frequency of dry days in the wet season o nset and cessation months of March (increasing by 2.8 –5.6 days per month) 
and October (increasing by 1.4 –1.7 days per month).  An increasing number of dry days per month will increase the risk 
of droughts occurring in northern Ghana.  
 
Climate change impacts7  
The Guina savanna  and Sudan savanna  agroecological zones of  northern Ghana, on which the livelihoods of 
smallholder farmers rely, are already under considerable stress because of inter alia : i) unfavourable climate conditions  
and trends ; ii) extensive land and environmental degradation related to increasing  population growth rates; and iii) 
unsustainable farming methods.    The effects of future climate change are expected to exacerbate the current problems 
in these systems through the negative impact pathways ( See Annex 2 – Section 5 - Error! Reference source not 
found.  
 
Impact on food systems:   Climate change is impacting dry season food production.   Few smallholder farmers have 
access to irrigation infrastructure and as a result are highly dependent on rain for their livelihoods.   As described above, 
the long duration of the dry season experienced in northern Ghana results in prolonged periods of food shortages. Dry 
season gardening can supplement food production outside of the major growing season, but  can only be practiced in 
areas with a nearby water source, where wat er is available even during the driest months of the year. However, climate 
change is exacerbating water shortages during the dry season. High temperatures in northern Ghana already contribute 
to reduced water -retention capacity and frequent drying of wate r bodies — such as wells, dams, and streams — that 
serve as sources of water for both livestock and household use, including for dry season gardening.  
 
Food security during the dry season is further impacted by the high rate of post -harvest losses (PHLs). Post -harvest 
losses in staple foods in Ghana such as maize, cassava, yam and rice amount to over 35%, 34%, 24% and 7% of 
yields, respectively. Increased temperatures linked to clima te change exacerbat e the problem through the expansion 
of ranges of the weeds, pests and fungi that cause food spoilage — as well as an increase in their multiplication rates. 
In addition to causing food spoilage, an increased rate of fungal growth results  in: i) reduced seed viability; ii) 
discolouration; iii) caking; iv) mouldy smells; and v) the production of mycotoxins in stored food.

8 Republic of Ghana. Ghana National Drought Plan. Available at https://knowledge.unccd.int/sites/default/files/inline -
files/1%20FINAL_NDP_Ghana.pdf.  
9 Available at: https://weadapt.org/knowledge -base/nature -based -solutions/bush -fires-for-natural -resources -and-
environmental -management -in-ghana -a-review/?utm_source=chatgpt.com   
10 Available at: https://fireecology.springeropen.com/articles/10.1186/s42408 -023-00198 -z?utm_source=chatgpt.com   Impact on water resources :  Decreasing wet -season length, coupled with rising temperatures, is increasing the 
frequency in which surface water bodies such as dams and streams are drying up. It is estimated that changing rainfall 
patterns in Ghana will lead to a general reduction in an nual river flow by 15 –20% for the year 2020 and 30 –40% for 
20508.  The groundwater system in Ghana is a dynamic system that mostly recharges through direct rainfall infiltration, 
especially in the northern regions.   The shallow aquafer ground water resources are already classified moderately 
vulnerable to flood and drought impacts. Increased temperatures, drought occurrence and potential evapotranspiration 
will reduce the resource recharge.  
 
Inversely, e xtreme rainfall events often result in severe flooding across many areas of northern Ghana, destroying 
crops, taking lives and damaging assets and public infrastructures. Numerous such extreme events have been 
observed over the past 60 years (Figure 4.7 of  Annex 2: Feasibility Study)  resulting in human deaths and displacement 
of 16 million people .   Floods have become increasingly frequent over the past 30 years  and is a significant risk to 
agriculture in flood -prone areas  in northern Ghana.  
 
Fluvial flooding  results in river overbank flow , and there expose crops and livestock to flood damage and loss, especially 
for those farmers farm ing close to the river due to a lack of irrigation systems , and in severe cases also affect houses 
and roads . Pluvial floods, also called flash floods, occur after heavy rainfall, often only in short time periods. Pluvial 
floods play a role in all districts. Flash floods result in crops and fertile soil being washed away. Breaches of small 
reservoir dams cause fl ood risk in all districts, especially when dams are poorly constructed, maintained or treat ed.  
 
The problem of reduced water availability in the dry season is further exacerbated by the increasing frequency and 
intensity of extreme rainfall events which lead to more eroded material and pollutants being deposited into water bodies, 
further decreasing water quality and reducing the reservoir storage capacity . Reduction in storage capacity of reservoirs 
in northern Ghana, in turn cause them to dry -up quicker in the dry season as well as increase the risk of dam collapse, 
leading to downstream flooding.  
  
Impact on local infrastructure and livelihoods :   Extreme climate events may undermine the effectiveness of existing 
rural infrastructure and the impacts on rural livelihoods and agricultural systems will thus also depend on their climate -
resilience and compatibility with changing climatic patterns. Extre me climate events have already caused important 
economic losses. According to EPA, 2007 floods in northern regions affected and displaced more than 300,000 people, 
damaged or destroyed almost 26,000 houses, collapsed 13 bridges, destroyed 1,060 km of feeder roads, contaminated 
634 drinking water facilities, amongst other damages to social infrastructure.  
 
Social infrastructure, including community buildings, are also vulnerable to the impacts of climate change. Recurrent 
floods, heavy downpours and long dry spells are the ways in which climatic change can affect social and community 
infrastructure. Houses, school buildings, water and sanitation facilities are infrastructures that are both sensitive and 
vulnerable to climate change.  
 
Impact on ecosystems:  Uncontrolled bushfires in northern Ghana are reducing the capacity of agroecosystems to 
provide critical ecosystem services such as  NTFPs, forage, soil stabilisation and flood mitigation. Bushfire impact 
agriculture through several pathways, including: i) causing extensive damage to crops and fruit trees; ii) reducing the 
fertility of farmlands by removing soil organic matter; and iii) causing vegetation loss  in fallowed/grazing lands and 
woodland.   The frequency and intensity of uncontrolled bushfires in northern Ghana is increasing. While the increase 
is largely driven by rapid population growth and the break -down of socio -cultural norms that used to regulate land in 
northern Ghana, climate change i s exacerbating the problem.  The extent of the impact  of uncontrolled bushfires in 
northern Ghana are estimated as follows : 
• Forest loss:  Research indicates that approximately 30% of Ghana's reserved forest area is destroyed by fire 
each year9. 
• Burned areas : Between 2001 and 2021, the Guinea -savanna and Forest -savanna mosaic zones experienced 
significant fire activity. For instance, in the Guinea -savanna zone, December recorded an average burned area of 
107.539 km² with 59,288 fires detected10.

Scaling up good adaptation practice12 
This project draw on past and ongoing programmes and projects which have generated positive adaptation results for 
beneficiaries in Ghana , the key one being the  sustainable land and water management (SLWM)  project to enhance soil 
and land productivity  and watershed services  for climate resilience in northern Ghana  (evaluation report at Annex 18) . 
 
 
B.2 (a). Theory of change narrative and diagram (max.  1500 words, approximately  3 pages  plus diagram ) 
Problem statement  
The agriculture sector in northern Ghana — which supports ~80% of the total workforce — is dominated by smallholder 
farmers practising mostly subsistence farming. Given that smallholder farms in northern Ghana are almost entirely 
rainfed — using mostly traditional agricultural practices and minimum techn ology — agricultural production is strongly 
dependent on the availability of natural resources, making them highly vulnerable to changes in their environment and 
the climate. The a groecological systems which underpin natural resource -based livelihoods , are notably  already under 
considerable stress because of inter alia : i) unfavourable climate conditions; ii) extensive environmental degradation 
related to high population growth rates, and traditional, unsustainable farming methods and iii) dam spills in Tamne 
reservoir and Bagre Dam in Burkina Faso . These baseline challenges are exacerbated by climate change, including: i) 
decreases in the duration of the wet season; ii) increases in rainfall variability, with less frequent but more intense 
rainfall events; iii) increases in the number of dry days; iv) increases in maximum and minimum temperatures, with 
more frequent heatwaves; v) increased evapotranspiration; and vi) a reduced annual moisture index.  
 
The impacts of these changes to climate on smallholder farmers include:  
• increased frequency and severity of floods — particularly in areas near rivers — that will lead to soil erosion, major 
crop losses and damage to agricultural infrastructure, including post -harvest storage and irrigation /water  facilities, 
and  transport infrastructure and small dams being breached;  
• increased frequency and intensity of droughts  leading to, among other things: i) increased frequency in the drying -
up of surface water bodies such as dams and streams; and  subsequent stress on water systems for both 
agriculture and human use; ii) a decline in agricultural productivity as the ability of small -scale farmers to engage 
in dry season gardening is reduced;  
• shortened agricultural production periods resulting in decreased crop yields and income as the number of 
achievable wet season cropping cycles and harvests of non -timber forest products (NTFPs) from agroecosystems 
are reduced;  
• pushing common northern Ghanaian crop and livestock varieties beyond their optimum thermal limits and 
expanding the ranges of weeds and pests that prefer warmer climates as previously cooler areas experience 
warmer temperatures;  
• increased soil erosion and loss of soil nutrients leading to decreased crop yields and increased pollution and 
eutrophication of water bodies; and  
• more frequent and severe uncontrolled bushfires resulting in the destruction of crop fields — particularly of the dry 
mature crop — and reduced capacity of agroecosystems to provide critical ecosystem services such as NTFPs, 
forage, soil stabilisation and flood mitigation.  
• Increased incidence of climate change induced disasters on the built environment and settlements, ultimately 
resulting in disruptions to normal human activities and economic losses in terms of asset damages.  
These impacts, when combined with the non -climate drivers of environmental degradation and dam spillages, have a 
substantial impact on the livelihoods of smallholder farmers and their households in northern Ghana, with subsequent 
impacts on food security and income generation of the country’s most vulnerable people. Without effective adaptation 
action  supported by access to timely climate information and analysis , northern Ghanaian smallholder farmers will likely 
remain vulnerable to the current and future effects of climate change, with serious repercussions for the northern 
Ghanaian economy, ultimately increasing the north -south divide in Ghana.  
 
An analysis of current drought and flood early warning services in Ghana indicates that the majority have incomplete 
coverage and limited channels for information transfer and dissemination. Several studies — including a gap analysis 
of drought and flood r isk management and early warnings in Ghana , a capacity needs assessment of the Ghana


---
*Page 14*
---

B 
 GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL  V.3.0 | PAGE 8 
 
 Meteorological Agency (GMet) , a Gender Assessment and Action Plan (Annex 8), and the assessments under the 
UNEP CLIMWARN project  — have also revealed several technological barriers to building the resilience in the 
agricultural sector to the impacts of c limate change, namely relating to information access. Stakeholder consultations 
(see Annex 7h: Stakeholder Engagement Plan) revealed that if women were provided improved technologies and 
financial services, they would likely invest in the land, leading to increased production — and potential increased income 
— which benefits not only themselves, but their families and communities too. Further to this, drought and flood 
information management and early warning systems have been identified as gaps by stakehol der consultations during 
the project development process (Annex 7h).  
 
Preferred adaptation solution  
The preferred adaptation solution would see a reduction in the vulnerability to climate change of smallholder farmers 
in northern Ghana through the implementation and adoption of climate -resilient agriculture, ecosystem -based 
adaptation  approaches, alternative climate -resilient livelihoods   and locally led investments in climate resilient rural 
infrastructure . These three suites of climate change adaptation interventions will complement one another to increase 
the resilience of smallholder farm ing communiti es to climate change impacts.  Climate -resilient Agriculture (CRA) is an 
integrative approach that assists with the reorientation and transformation of agricultural systems to ensure food 
security and environmental sustainability in a changing climate . Climate -resilient agricultural techniques will enhance 
agricultural productivity under future climate changes conditions (both crop production and livestock). Ecosystem -based 
adaptation interventions (such as riverbank restoration, agroforestry, reforestat ion and fire management) will enhance 
the supply of ecosystem goods and services, further increasing agricultural productivity (for example through reduced 
soil erosion and increased fodder for livestock) while also preventing land degradation and thereby red ucing the impacts 
of floods and droughts. The enhanced supply of ecosystem goods and services will, in turn, provide communities with 
options for alternative climate -resilient livelihoods, reducing their need to engage in unsustainable practices such as 
expanding slash and burn agriculture to generate income.   The realization of locally relevant climate resilient 
infrastructure investments will reduce direct losses of damages and indirect costs of disruption, while contributing to 
build and sustain climate -resilient communities and environments.  Investments in key gaps in forecast and observing 
equipment in Northern Ghana will transform the skill of weather forecasts.  Climate advisories for drought and flood 
hazards will support famers to  optimise planting methods and schedules for higher yields . 
These climate change adaptation intervention s — targeted to circumvent and/or disrupt northern Ghana -specific 
climate change impacts — would be introduced to smallholder farmer communities in a coordinated manner throughout 
the three northern regions of Ghana.  The implementation of these climate change adaptation interventions will scale 
up and strengthen EbA, combined with associated community training, extension services and awareness -raising, 
would increase opportunities for knowledge and technology exc hange between communities, and thereby promote 
autonomous upscaling of these interventions. Technical assistance (TA), capacity building and performance incentives  
to District Assemblies will  further promote the upscaling and sustainability of these interventions, adaptation will be 
integrated into regional and district development plans, and the incremental costs for adaptation absorbed through 
earmarked financial top ups to regular districts budgets.  With improved capacity, sub -national administrations, in 
particular District Assemblies, will be better placed to access additional national and international climate finance as 
well. This should in principle lead to integrated climate change responses at the local level and provide the continuity 
and mainten ance capacity that local infrastructure and governance interventions often lack. Overall, these approaches 
would transition the smallholder farming communities of northern Ghana away from unsustainable agricultural and land -
use practices, while promoting m ore climate -resilient, productive and sustainable livelihoods.  
 
Project objective  
The proposed project objective is to enhance the climate resilience of vulnerable smallholder farming communities in 
northern Ghana by improving food security and enhancing the agro -based rural economy.  
 
Project outcomes  
The project ’s outcomes will  strengthen institutional and regulatory systems for climate -responsive planning and 
development, enabling government agencies and local institutions to effectively integrate climate adaptation into 
policies and strategies. The project supports the adoption of climate -resilient agricultural practices, alternative 
livelihoods, and access to financial resources, ensuring farmers can sustainably adapt to climate impacts. Additionally, 
the project improves knowledge and learning p rocesses, fostering informed de cision -making and empowering 
communities with the tools and information needed to scale up climate adaptation efforts across the region.  
 
Barriers to the adaptation solution and project objective

15 E.g. staff from the MESTI, MoFA, EPA, MLGCRA , MoF and the NDA.  Several barriers to the preferred adaptation solution have been identified for northern Ghana but can be categorised 
into three main barriers. These are listed below and explained in detail in Section 7 of Annex 2: Feasibility Study .  
• Barrier 1:  Limited  institutional, technical and financial capacity for planning, budgeting, implementing and monitoring 
climate change adaptation interventions.  
• Barrier 2:   Limited knowledge and awareness  of climate resilient methods and ecosystem linkages in disaster 
impact;  
• Barrier 3: Inadequate access for communities to climate information and best practices for applying early warning 
data in agricultural practices.  
• Barrier 3:  Limited access for local governments and communities to financial resources to finance adaption 
interventions.  
 
Project approach to overcoming the barriers to adaptation  
The proposed project will overcome the abovementioned barriers to increase the climate resilience of smallholder 
farming communities in northern Ghana. This will be achieved through five interconnected outputs, described in detail 
in Section 1 of Annex 2: feasibility Study . The overall theory of change for this project is presented in Figure 1 below.  
 
Barrier 1 will be addressed by strengthening the technical and institutional capacity of regional and district government 
structures in northern Ghana to plan, budget, implement and monitor climate change adaptation interventions. The 
policy work will take place in year 4 -5 of the project, once results can be harvested and used for planning purposes.  
The EPA will execute this output, based on experience gathered in the execution of the country’s National Adaptation 
Planning process, due to be completed in 2025.  
 
Training on climate -resilient agricultural practices, EbA and alternative climate -resilient livelihoods will be provided to 
extension officers (Activity 2.1) and local communities (Activity 2.2), thereby addressing Barrier 2. Extension officers 
and local communities will implement these climate change adaptation interventions, allowing them to gain practical 
knowledge of these practices (Activity 2.4). Thirdly, training materials and knowledge products capturing best practices 
and lessons learned, including  those related to the use of fire by communities from other initiatives such as the SLWMP, 
AF and IFAD projects will be disseminated (Activity 5.1), allowing knowledge of climate change adaptation to be shared 
with institutions and individuals who were not directly involved in project implementation. Finally, the project will conduct  
community -level knowledge -sharing and awareness -raising events ( Activity 5.2) for both beneficiary and non-
beneficiary communities in northern Ghana. By increasing the knowledg e of climate change adaptation in non -
beneficiary communities, these events will promote the autonomous upscaling of climate change interventions 
promoted by the project.  
 
Barrier 2 will be addressed by convening of knowledge -sharing and awareness -raising events (Activity 5.2) with 
participants from: i) district -level government staff13; ii) regional -level government staff14; iii) national -level government   
staff15; iv) climate change adaptation project and programme managers; v) representatives of academic and research 
institutions; vi) NGO staff; vii) representatives of multilateral development agencies; and viii) project beneficiaries. 
These events will promote i nformation sharing and encourage coordination between current and future climate change 
adaptation projects and programmes.  A robust monitoring and evaluation strategy will be put into place (Activity 5.1) 
to future facilitate the generation and dissemination of knowledge products (Activity 5. 2).   
 
Barrier 3 will be addressed by the new data information and management system established under Activity 1.2. The 
existing drought early warning systems in Ghana have limited coverage and inadequate channels for information 
transfer and dissemination. As a result of these limitations, smallholder farmers in northern Ghana are unable to apply 
climate informatio n to their agricultural practices and take proactive measures again drought impacts. By providing 
these farmers with quality climate data and early wa rning against drought, the project will enable a shift away from 
reactive methods for drought management — which currently contribute to crop losses, thereby increasing climate 
vulnerability.  
 
The project will contribute to overcoming Barrier 4 by increasing the capacity of the most vulnerable smallholder farmers 
to form farmer -based organisations (FBOs), strengthening financial management capacity, links to value chains being 
developed in paral lel thereby facilitating linkages to agricultural credit and insurance (Activities 4.1 –4.4).  The 
implementation of climate -resilient agricultural practices (Activity 2.4) will generate consistently higher incomes for 
smallholder farmers, allowing them to finance adaptation interventions once the project has finished. Approximately

 US$270,000 and US$1.7 million in income is expected to be generated by investments in climate resilient agriculture

Together, the interventions mentioned above overcome the barriers to climate change adaptation in northern Ghana, 
thereby contributing to a paradigm shift within the agricultural sector towards climate -resilient sustainable development.  
 
 
Figure 1: Theory of change diagram  
The project will work at three levels: community level on planning and implementation systems as well as  at the 
Regional and National level regarding investments in weather forecast capability that will serve the Northern Sector, 
complementing investments in forecasting capability in the South of the country  as well as  establishing a national 
action plan for drought and flood hazard management .  The community level integrated ecosystem -based adaptation 
approach will inform the District level planning and budgeting systems. The project will build capacity and awareness 
of District planning officers to  scale up  the project approach. C ombined with associated community training, extension 
services and awareness -raising, this will  increase opportunities for knowledge and technology exchange between 
communities, and thereby promote autonomous upscaling of these interventions.  
Specific efforts will be made to support women farmers and female -led households who are particularly vulnerable to 
climate impacts due to limited land tenure and financial access.  Farmer -based organi sations will be trained in financial 
literacy and savings management with a focus on empowering women and creating equitable access to credit and 
insurance schemes. Through this financial strengthening, FBOs, particularly  those led by women, will enable the farmer 
groups to connect to the local banking sector thereby promoting higher returns that can be reinvested.

B.3. Project/programme description (max. 2500 words, approximately 5 pages)  
Geographies of intervention and target beneficiaries:  
The proposed project will be implemented in the North  East, Upper East and Upper West Regions of northern Ghana. 
As described above, the five northern regions are especially exposed to negative climate change impacts. Additionally, 
the northern populace is extremely sensitive to climate variability and change and has limited capacity to adapt to any 
changes in climate16. It is for these reasons of excessive exposure, elevated sensitivity and limited adaptive capacity 
that the northern regions of Ghana are considered the most vulnerable regions of the country to climate change17. See 
Section 3 of Annex 2 — Feasibility Study,18 for a detailed analysis that identifies the three northern regions as the most 
vulnerable to climate change impacts.  
 
Within the northern regions of Ghana, implementation of the proposed project will be focused on eight of the 42 districts 
that comprise northern Ghana), namely:  
• Jirapa , Lambussie , Lawra and Wa West in the Upper West Region ;  
• Binduri  and Garu in the Upper East Region; and  
• East Mamprusi and Yunyoo -Nasuan in the North  East Region . 
 
These districts were selected for project implementation using a comprehensive, quantitative assessment of district -
specific vulnerability to climate change — incorporating exposure, sensitivity and adaptive capacity of communities — 
and a rigorous stakeho lder engagement process (see Section 3 of Annex 2 : Feasibility Study, and Annex 7 h: 
Stakeholders  Engagement Plan).  
 
The districts are partly in the Sudan Savanna Zone, located in the north -eastern corner of Ghana, with the majority of 
the zone located in Burkina Faso and Mali. It is characterised by fire -swept short grasses interspersed with low -density


---
*Page 18*
---

B 
 GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL  V.3.0 | PAGE 12 
 
  
19 i.e. high exposure and sensitivity and low adaptive capacity.  
20 <5km  woodland. Grass cover is low, and some areas of land are bare and severely eroded. The Sudan Savanna Zone 
receives the least rainfall of Ghana’s agro -ecological zones (~940 mm per annum) and only one rainfall season. Other 
Districts are in the Guinea Savan na Zone (or Interior Savanna Zone), located north of the Transitional Zone.  This is 
the largest agro -ecological zone in Ghana, covering the northern half of the country. The zone is characterised by 
wooded grassland, consisting of a ground cover of grasse s of variable height, interspersed with fire -resistant, 
deciduous, broad -leaved trees. In general, tree cover and height decrease from south to north along a gradient of 
decreasing rainfall. The Guinea Savanna Zone receives ~1,100 mm of rainfall per year, mostly during one rainfall 
season.  
 
From those districts not currently receiving support, and taking climate change vulnerability scores into account, the 
final project districts were selected by national, regional and district level stakeholders from: i) the National Designated 
Authority (NDA) of Ghana within the Ministry of Finance (MoF); ii) members of the inter -ministerial Technical Advisory  
Committee (TAC) to the NDA; iii) staff of the Directorate of Crop Services from the Ministry of Food and Agriculture 
(MoFA); iv) representatives of the Environmental Protection Agency (EPA) from the Ministry of Environment, Science, 
Technology and Innovat ion (MESTI); v)  Ministry of Local Government, Chieftaincy and Religious Affairs  (MLG CRA );  
and vi) members of civil society organisations.

support from the SLWMP and Adaptation Fund projects and thus were not considered eligible for selection.  
 
Within each district, 15 communities will receive direct support from the project. Each community will receive support 
for three consecutive years (Table 1). Across the eight districts, the project will, therefore, support 120 communities 
over seven years. The direct beneficiary communities will be selected based on a rigorous set of selection criteria and 
comprehensive consultations at the national, regional , district and community levels  (see Annex 7h: Stakeholder 
Engagement Plan ). The selection criteria include inter alia : i) high vulnerability to climate change19; ii) proximity20 to at 
least five other vulnerable communities to promote knowledge and technology transfer to these vulnerable

At the time of writing, four communities within each district have been selected based on the criteria described above. 
These communities were selected following detailed community consultations ( see Annex 7 h: Stakeholder 
Engagement Plan and Section E.5.3) that took place during the development of this Funding Proposal. Three of the 
four communities already identified will be selected to receive support during the first year of project implementation. 
The remaining community, as well as the additional communities that will also be identified through the selection 
process described above, will receive support from the second year of project  implementation onwards.  
 
To ensure adaptive lear ning from implementation when moving from one commu nity to the next (Error! Reference 
source not found. 2) lessons learned will be systematically captur ed from each community after the first three years 
of direct support and applying those insights to subsequent communities. The project implementation team will conduct 
evaluations with community leaders, local authorities and participants to assess the effec tiveness of interventions, 
identify challenges and document successful practices, particularly those related to climate vulnerability reduction, 
knowledge transfer, and land access. These findings will inform adjustments to the selection criteria, support strategies 
and community engagement methods, ensuring that the approach evolves and improves as the project progresses 
across the 120 communities. This iterative learning process will foster a dynamic, responsive project implementation 
that becomes more ta ilored to local contexts over time.  
 
Project objective:  
The proposed project objective is to enhance the climate resilience of vulnerable smallholder farming communities in 
northern Ghana by improving food security and enhancing the agro -based rural economy22. 
 
The project will be implemented in eight districts in northern Ghana that have been specifically chosen because: i) of 
their high vulnerability to climate change impacts ( see Annex 2: Feasibility Study, Section 3); and ii) they are currently 
not receiving support from other ongoing initiatives.  
 
The project’s objective will be achieved by the successful implementation of project activities to achieve f ive outputs 
comes as outlined in the Theory of Change and detailed in Section 13 of Annex 2: Feasibility Study  and summarised 
below : 
 
Component 1:   Early warning systems

Component 2: Climate -resilient livelihoods  
 
Output 2: Climate -resilient agricultural practices, EbA and alternative climate -resilient livelihoods implemented in 
beneficiary communities.  
The proposed project will strengthen capacity for and implement climate -resilient agriculture, landscape restoration for 
drought and flood management and alternative climate -resilient livelihoods in beneficiary communities.  The proposed 
project will focus on training of 200 extension officers (of which 30% of women) on climate -resilient agricultural 
practices, EbA and alternative climate -resilient livelihoods, and support the implementation of appropriate climate 
change adaptation interventions drawn from a menu  of possible interventions identified from climate risk assessments  
and by  beneficiary communities  with an aim  to reduce their vulnerability to climate change. Each beneficiary community 
will be trained on climate change interventions and will develop/finalise their own Climate Change Adaptation Plan 
(CCAP ). The specific adaptation interventions identified within the CCAP – drawn from the menu of climate change 
adaptation interventions (See section 13, Annex 2: Feasibility Study) – will then be implemented in each community 
with the assistance of district extension officers.  
 
Activity 2.1: Train extension officers on climate -resilient agricultural practices, EbA and alternative climate -resilient 
livelihoods.  
Activity 2.2: Train beneficiary communities in northern Ghana on climate -resilient agricultural practices, EbA and 
alternative climate -resilient livelihoods.  
Activity 2.3: Develop community climate action plans (CCAPs) in collaboration with beneficiary communities.   
Activity 2.4: Implement climate change adaptation interventions, including climate -resilient agricultural practices, EbA 
and alternative climate -resilient livelihoods, identified in the CCAPs in beneficiary communities.  
Activity 2.5: Develop a monitoring and evaluation strategy for climate advisory services in northern Ghana to improve 
the accuracy and appropriateness of advisories for smallholder farmers.  
 
Output 3:   Restoration of landscape to reduce drought and flood risk.  
At the farm level, a variety of nature -based solutions or nature -based infrastructure can be implemented, including 
reforestation efforts through tree planting to reduce soil erosion and increase infiltration capacity and establishing 
riparian buffer zones  along the riverside to reduce flood impacts. Encouraging the adaptation of flood -based farming 
measures to store flood water to be used during dry spells and drain the land after rainfall or floods will preserve 
agricultural production.   During the proje ct lifespan, agroforestry and restoration interventions will be implemented in 
120 communities. Estimating ~1000 people per community, the direct beneficiaries of these interventions have been 
calculated by assuming that all 1000 individuals in each of the  120 target communities will benefit.    In addition, 2000 
hectares of riparian land in the 8 Districts will be restore d to protect against floods23.

Output 4: Increased access of smallholder farmers to financial resources and engagement with the private sector.  
Output 4 will bridge this gap by increasing the capacity of the most vulnerable smallholder farmers , such as women  
farmers , to form farmer -based organisations (FBOs ) and manage finances and thereafter access agricultural credit and 
insurance.  The project will connect the local level demand for credit to international blended finance initiatives which 
UNEP is brokering. In doing so, this output will contribute to GCF Outc ome A7.0 — Strengthened adaptive capacity 
and reduced exposure to climate risks. GCF proceeds will not be used to finance credit lines and guarantees through 
financial intermediaries.  
 
Technical assistance will be provided  by GIRSAL a Government non-banking financial institution established to de -risk 
agricultural financing and stimulate increased lending to the agricultural sector,  with the project reimbursement of 
experts’ time and logistical costs (rental of training venue and catering).  GIRSAL confirmed its interest in partnering 
for the project  and agreed to lead the technical assistance to local FIs on the benefits of EBA/CRA for the 
creditworthiness of farmers .  
 
In order to c reate a credit score database on FBOs and their members that are targeted by the project and have never 
borrowed before , an entity such as Financial Access, an NGO with specific expertise in the creation of smallholder 
farmer credit databases  would be partnered with the EPA and the project.  The entity would also train LFIs on its scoring 
methodology and use of the database. Financial Access has already confirmed its strong interest in creating a credit 
score database for both farmers and FBOs targeted by the project, to be made available as an online dashboar d to 
LFIs. Financial Access could also provide technical assistance to LFIs on the Financial Access scoring methodology 
and use of the dashboard.  
 
Activity 4.1: Establish farmer -based organisations (FBOs) and Village Savings and Loans Associations (VSLAs)  that 
can access credit and insurance for farming and non -farming livelihood activities.  
Activity 4.2: Connect FBOs and local financial institutions to improve access of beneficiary communities to credit and 
insurance  products.  
Activity 4.3: Establish a blended finance model to provide credit lines to support climate -resilient agriculture.  
 
 
Output 5: Knowledge and awareness of climate threats to agricultural livelihoods and available adaptation options 
increased to inform the upscaling of climate change adaptation across northern Ghana.  
To promote the upscaling and replication of project interventions, Output 5 will focus on activities that contribute to 
knowledge sharing and awareness -raising at national and local levels. By promoting the upscaling and replication of 
project interventions outside of beneficiary communities, the project will facilitate widespread and sustained behavioural 
transformation in smallholder farming communities in northern Ghana. The project will specifically integrate findings 
and experiences of two other approv ed GCF project : the UNDP -Forestry Commission project  which  does not have a 
knowledge management output and the AfDB project which has a small activity on knowledge management.  The 
proposed pro ject will  increase the prominence of this output in its proposal by adding an economic valuation component 
to measure ecosystem services arising from all three GCF projects, drawing on the methodologies and experiences 
from our Environmental Economics work.  Furthermore, the drought and flood management plan at the national level  
(Output 1)  will feature as part of awareness and training workshops with District Assemblies in order to  mainstream  in 
the 8 District Medium Term Development Plans and will be used as part o f the instruments to guide the preparation of 
the CCAPs at the community level as an integral part of the CCAPs .   
 
Activity 5.1: Generate and disseminate knowledge products capturing best practice and lessons learned to inform the 
upscaling of climate change adaptation across northern Ghana.  
Activity 5.2: Conduct knowledge -sharing and awareness -raising events .

The proposed project will be implemented over a seven -year period . The United Nations Environment Programme 
(UNEP) will serve as the Accredited Entity (AE)  and the following entities will serve as the Executing Entities (EEs) : 
i) Government of Ghana represented by  the Ministry of Environment, Science and Tech nology (MESTI) delegating to 
the Environment Protection Agency (EPA) , which will provide overall lead coordinating and executing role for the 
project, and  for Activities  2.1 2.2, 2.3, 2.4 , 2.5 , in close collaboration and coordination with the Ministry of Food and 
Agriculture (MOFA) as implementing partner (IP), and lead on Activities  3.1, 4.1, 4.2, 4.3, 5.1, 5.2  and 5. 3; ii). 
Government of Ghana represented by the Ministry of Communication  and Digitalization’s  Ghana Meteorological 
Agency (GM ET) for Activities 1.1, 1,2,1.3,1.4,1.5  . with the UNEP -Danish Hydraulic Institute (DHI) providing technical 
advisory services and Ghana’s Water Resources Commission serving as implementing partner (IP)  for national and 
district level engagement and coordination . 
 
 
Accredited Entity  
As the Accredited Entity , UNEP will carry out fiduciary and safeguards oversight and provide the necessary scientific 
expertise and technical support to the project formulation, start up, implementation, evaluations and closure.  In 
addition, a UNEP representative will fulfil the role of Task Manager (TM) , taking responsibility for project oversight and 
supervision , including undertaking at least two missions per year to the project . As AE, UNEP will ensure that the 
project activities are aligned with both GCF and UNEP’s policies. Operating policies and procedures will follow UNEP’s 
programme manual, which includes provisions for financial management and procurement . 
 
UNEP is implementing several large integrated programmes funded by the GEF (the largest being USD98M for the net 
zero, nature positive accelerator integrated programme).   We have also implemented several projects in Ghana as 
part of regional and global pr ojects.   UNEP has extensive experience in the design and implementation of initiatives 
that promote EbA — which delivers adaptation as well as mitigation benefits — and has championed tools and 
methodologies that the proposed project will rely on at a nati onal and global level. These include Vulnerability Impact 
Assessments, Natural Capital Accounting, among others.  An example of UNEP’s operating experience in the region is 
the Stimulating Community Initiatives in Sustainable Land Management project (SCI -SLM, 2009 –2014)24. The overall 
objective of the SCI -SLM was to refine ways of stimulating the improvement and upscaling of community -based 
sustainable land management initiatives and to institutionally embed SCI -SLM approaches at local and regional levels 
in four pilot cou ntries in Africa, including Ghana. UNEP was also active in Ghana under the  Federal Ministry for the 
Environment, Nature Conservation, Building and Nuclear Safety (BMUB)  UNEP/UNDP/WRI  GCF Readiness 
Programme. Under this programme, UNEP supported Ghana in capacity development with regards to National 
Designated Authority (NDA) capacity and decision -making about project prioritisation. It also supported national entity 
accreditation capacity building, as well as the accreditation application submission and was  selected as an AE for this 
project when it was determined that national accreditation might delay the submission of this proposal. Specific projects 
implemented by the UNEP in Ghana include inter alia : i) addressing transboundary concerns in the Volta River basin 
and its downstream coastal area (2007 –2012)25; and ii) CC -DARE — Climate Change and Development — Adapting 
by Reducing Vulnerability (2008 –2012)26. In addition to the aforementioned projects, UNEP is the Delivery Partner for 
Ghana’s NAP Readiness project GHA -RS-003 “Enhancing multi -sector planning and capacity for effective adaptation 
in Ghana”. UNEP’s central role in Ghana’s NAP process allows for close alignment of the proposed project with the 
country’s adaptation processes, including the central - and district -level systems for adaptation planning being 
established through the NAP Readiness project. Where possible, existing structures established under the NAP 
Readiness project will be used by the proposed project to maximise efficiency and collaboration .  The project will also 
benefit from UNEP’s Regional office for Africa’s participation in the UN Country Team (UNCT) monthly coordination 
meetings, hosted by the UN Resident Coordinator Office, with the aim of ensuring effective coordination of UN 
programmes and projects executed by Government ministries and agencies with support from UN agencies (e.g. 
UNDP, FAO, etc.), and benefit from lesson learned and avoid duplication of efforts.

27 The PCA will determine requirements for the disbursement and management of funds to the EE, as well as establish 
agreed upon supervisory roles.  The Environmental Protection Agency (EPA) of the Ministry of Environment, Science, Technology and Innovation 
(MESTI) will serve as the main Executing Entity  (EE) of the project.  The EPA is the lead institution for UNFCCC 
activities in the country and is the main Country Implementation Institution for the technical coordination of activities on 
climate change and other environmental conventions ratified by Ghana. The EPA has a proven record as an executing 
entity in Ghana, having been instrumental in the imp lementation of large -scale environmental programmes, including 
inter alia  the GEMP and SLWMP.  
 
The Ghana Meteorological Agency (GMet) will implement Output 1. GMet have technical expertise with respect to 
climate variability, climate change, seasonal forecasting and drought assessmen t.  
 
UNEP will sign  separate  Project Cooperation Agreement s (PCA s) with the EPA  and GMet to establish clear roles and 
responsibilities for the execution of project activities27; and to ensure that the project is executed in line with GCF and 
UNEP rules, policies and requirements. The PCAs will establish clear roles and responsibilities for the respective parties 
for the delivery of the proposed activities, the schedule and conditions for instalments, the determination of the 
prevailing fiduciary standards and the terms and conditio ns for arbitrations and termination of contract. The PCAs will 
include specific obligations for the EEs on project execution, financial management, personnel administration and 
reporting, as well as on arbitration and liability terms. The EEs will be requi red to comply with UNEP rules, policies and 
procedures on procurement. The EPA  and GMet will be accountable to the AE for the effective use of resources during 
project implementation . The EEs will supervise, oversee and manage project implementation, as well as report on 
project progress; and to ensure that project activities are well coordinated and aligned with national priorities. UNEP, 
as Accredited Entity for the project, has under taken a capacity assessment of the EPA particularly by reviewing the 
elements of governance and public accountability.  UNEP  is confident that the EPA  has the relevant fiduciary and 
financial management capacity to act as the EE. All operating procedures will align with the UNEP’s programme 
manual, which includes provisions for financial management and procurement.   GMET and EPA will sign an 
Implementation Agreement  that sets out the reporting relationship (GMET to EPA) as well r esponsibilities on delivering 
project targets.  
UNEP will also sign a PCA with the UNEP -DHI Centre on Water and Environment (UNEP -DHI Centre)  which is  a United 
Nations Environment Programme (UNEP) centre of expertise. UNEP -DHI Centre is dedicated to supporting UNEP’s 
work in helping national governments improve the management, development and use of their freshwater resources in 
the face of climate chal lenges. The UNEP -DHI Centre is hosted by DHI, an independent, not -for- profit, international 
advisory and research organization. DHI has more than 60 year s of experience in water resources management and 
is represented by over 1,000 staff in more than 25 countries. For the upcoming project, UNEP -DHI Centre will draw 
upon DHI’s cutting edge hydrological and climatic modelling  tools, which are globally recognized and used by 
governments and leading international organizations in many regions of the world.  UNEP UNEP -DHI was the 
implementing partner in the pilot project that looked at the flood and drought early warning system for the Volta Basin, 
covering Benin, Burkina Faso, Cote D’Ivoire, Ghana, Mali and Togo, which ended in 2017.  UNEP -DHI is now providi ng 
technical assistance to a project focused on reversing the deterioration of ecosystems and water resources in the Volta 
River Basin. The goal of the project is to support integrated ecosystem -based development in the Volta River Basin by 
enhancing transboundary governance and restoring and conserving ecosystems to ensure sustainable livelihoods. 
Funded by the Global Environm ent Facility (GEF) through UN Environment Programme and IUCN, the five -year project 
is being implemented by the Volta Basin Authority (VBA) and the Global Water Partnership -West Africa (GWP -WA) 
between 2023 -2028.  
 
 
Structure for implementation and governance  
 
At the national level, the EPA will receive guidance from a Project Steering Committee  (PSC) and technical advice 
from the National Climate Change Committee  (NCCC). The PSC will be responsible for providing project oversight 
and advisory support for project implementation. This includes reviewing annual work plans and project reports, and 
approving changes to project targets, activities or timelines. Bi -annua l PSC meetings — along with ad hoc  meetings 
when deemed necessary — will be held to take management decisions, discuss project performance against indicators 
and provide technical/strategic guidance to the Project Management Unit (PMU). The PSC  will be co -chaired by MESTI 
and UNEP and comprise representatives from the: i) MoFA ; ii) Ministry of Finance (MoF); iii) Forestry Commission; iv)

particular, the FPO — in cooperation with  the UNEP Finance Management Officer (FMO)  — will be responsible for 
managing all financial transactions, assuring alignment with project work plans and national priorities.  
 
National, regional and local stakeholders  
At the regional (sub -national) level, the project will coordinate with  three regional EPA offices and Department of 
Agriculture offices located in Nalerigu  (North  East Region), Bolgatanga (Upper East Region) and Wa (Upper West 
Region). In each region, the EPA and Department of Agriculture will receive guidance from Local Steering Committees 
(LSCs) comprising the: i) Regional Coordinating Council; ii) Regional Planning Of fice; iii) Regional Director of 
Agriculture; iv) Regional Director of the EPA; v) Distri ct Coordinating Directors of project districts; vi) District Directors 
of Agriculture of project districts; vii) Regional Fire Officers; viii) District Forest Officers; and ix) District Planning O fficers 
of the project districts. Additional members may be included in the LSCs if and when required.  
 
The EPA will establish a n Implementation Agreement (IA)  with the Ministry of Food and Agriculture (MoFA) with regards 
to the management and execution of agricultural interventions.   A Project Implement  Manual will be established that 
defines roles and mandates of the respective parties to execute the project in accordance with the financing proposal. 
The MoFA Agricultural Extension Agents will be the primary link between district government structures and beneficiary 
communities.  This will include capacity building activities from the respective institutions for farming communities, as 
well as feedback and inputs from farming communities to project implementing parties in shaping the information 
services and their delivery modes to target the needs of farmers. Utilising the extension services communication 
channels will be particularly important given their importance in communicating technological advances and available  
resources from the national level institutions to the local community level (further details are provided in Annex 2: 
Feasibility Study). The need to equip extension officers with better climate information that can then be transferred to 
local communitie s has been echoed in community level consultations with farmers. In beneficiary communities, Climate 
Change Action Groups (CCAGs) and Farmer Based Organisations (FBOs) will provide a link between community 
members and district -level government structures and will serve to coordinate community -level activities.  
 
The GM et will establish a n Implementation Agreement (IA)  with the Water Resources Commission (WRC) with 
regards to integrating  hydrological information , in particular groundwater mo nitoring, into early warnings made 
available to facilitate proactive flood and drought hazard management.  A Project Implement Manual will be 
established that defines roles and mandates of the respective parties to execute the project in accordance with the 
financing proposal.

B.5. Justification for GCF funding request (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)  
Building the climate -resilience of the Ghanaian agricultural sector is vital to protect  the livelihoods of smallholder farmers 
in northern Ghana. Shifting rainfall and temperature regimes are expected to reduce already suboptimal crop yields. 
Consequently, food security and income generation of the country’s most vulnerable people will be negatively impacted. 
These negative impacts will be compounde d by — and further enhance — severe environmental degradation resulting 
from rapid population growth, dependen ce on natural resources and the use of unsustainable agricultural techniques. 
Without effective adaptation action, smallholder farmers will likely become increasingly vulnerable to the current and 
future effects of climate change, with serious repercussion s for both the northern and Ghanaian economy. However, 
climate change adaptation in Ghana is hampered by a lack of financial resources, institutional and technical capacity 
needed to implement effective adaptation interventions.  
 
Without investment into adaptation interventions, the livelihoods of ~ 10 million men and ~ 12 million women  (~75% of 
the population) who are reliant on agriculture will remain at risk to the threats of climate change.  However, the lack of 
financial resources hampers climate change adaptation in Ghana. The agricultural sector in Ghana is reliant on foreign 
funding, with ~65% of total public expenditure in support of food and agriculture funded by external sources. These 
include, among others, the World Bank, USAID an d the European Union30. The need for external funding is because 
of the current state of the Ghanaian economy. Despite a period of rapid economic growth between 2000 and 2013, 
graduating Ghana to lower -middle -income status, the country’s economy has seen a decline in growth ove r the past 
seven years31. Increasing debt, high inflation and currency depreciation have contributed to the deterioration of the 
economy, leading to a current account deficit of -9.3%32 and a debt -to-GDP ratio of 58%33. It is estimated that without 
interventions, the cumulative damages of weather events in four of the eight implementation districts will reach 
US$680.2 million by 2050 (elaborated in Annex 3: Economic and Financial Analysis). These damages will further 
constrain the GoG’s fiscal space for financing adaptation and resilience interventions. Additional challenges to Ghana’s 
economic outlook include high domestic financing costs, technical problems in the oil and gas sectors, energy problems


---
*Page 27*
---

B 
 GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL  V.3.0 | PAGE 21 
 
  
34 World Bank – 2017 – Ghana Country Overview. Available at: http://www.worldbank.org/en/country/ghana/overview .  
35 Sova C, Chaudhury AS, Nelson W, Nutsukpo DK, Zougmoré RB. 2014. Climate change adaptation policy in Ghana: 
Priorities for the agriculture sector.  
36 Mosello B, Adamtey R, Obuobie E (eds). 2017. Making water infrastructure investment decisions in a changing climate. A 
political economy study of river basin development in Ghana . Overseas Develoment Institute. London.  
37 Ibid.  
38 Ibid.  
39 Fearon J. 2000. Economic analysis of soil conservation practices in northern Ghana. MPhil, University of Ghana, Accra.  
40 Antwi -Agyei P. 2012. Vulnerability and adaptation of Ghana's food production systems and rural livelihoods to climate 
variability. DPhil, University of Leeds, Leeds.  
41 Gallenstein RA, Mishra K, Sam AG, Miranda MJ. 2019. Willingness to pay for insured loans in northern Ghana. Journal of 
Agricultural Economics  70: 640 -662. related to state -owned enterprises and continued weak commodity prices34. These factors have caused an 
unfavourable fiscal climate for the Ghanaian government, resulting in its inability to fund or secure private -sector loan 
financing for large -scale investments in innovative climate change adaptation measures. Therefore, give n the current 
economic situation in Ghana, the contribution of funds by the GCF is critical for the successful implementation of the 
proposed project. Without investment into climate -resilient agriculture and EbA interventions, the livelihoods of the 
~75% of the population of northern Ghana that are reliant on smallholder farming are at risk to the threats of climate 
change.  
 
Funds are being requested by the GoG in the form of a grant without repayment contingency, given the focus of the 
project on the most vulnerable communities in Ghana. The grant funds will be used to provide the inputs necessary for 
project implementation w hile the EPA and MOFA  will bear a large share of the project staffing costs. Grant finance is 
needed to fund the strengthening of technical and institutional capacity to develop, implement and coordinate climate 
change adaptation at regional and district level in northern Ghan a because of inadequate financial resources35. 
Although District Assemblies generate some revenues on their own (i.e. through district taxation), they barely manage 
to collect sufficient funds to cope with the many development priorities they face36. This is especially so in northern 
Ghana where many of the inhabitants depend on subsistence rain -fed agriculture for their livelihood37. The limited 
budgets are spent on priority projects, such as health, road construction and education with inadequate resources 
available for additional activities such as climate change adaptation38. Given the current economic situation in Ghana, 
the contribution of funds by the GCF is critical for the successful implementation of the proposed project.  
 
Given the  focus of the project on the most vulnerable communities in Ghana , there is currently limited investment from 
the private sector in these approaches. Consequently, northern Ghana has limited access to the financial resources 
necessary for the adoption of appropriate climate -resilient practices to adapt to climate change . Smallholder farmers 
are unable to leverage private sector finance as financial institutions are unwilling to grant loans to them for agricultural 
activities  because of the high -risk nature of rain -fed agriculture in the region39,40. Instead, the banks employ joint liability 
lending structures and lend  to registered farmer -based organisations (FBOs)41.  
 
The sustainable development of northern Ghana’s agricultural sector requires substantial investment into adaptation 
practices such as climate -resilient agriculture and EbA to build the climate -resilience of vulnerable farming 
communities. The initial returns of such investments are largely at the individual level, with returns being re -investe d 
by farmers into agricultural production and used to cover their basic needs, leaving limited scope for cost recovery for 
early investors. A GCF grant is, therefore, being sought by the GoG to invest in climate change adaptation and bring 
about a paradigm  shift in the agro -based rural economy of northern Ghana. Through this project, GCF support will 
accelerate northern Ghana’s transition towards climate -resilient sustainable development, facilitating economic growth 
and securing the livelihoods of vulnerab le smallholder farming communities.  
 
The Government of Ghana has secured co -financing to the value of US$ 6,987,681  through in -kind contributions and 
inclusive of a cash co -financing of US$ 250,000 . In-kind contributions consistent of staff time, offices and equipment 
that will be provided by the EPA and Directorate of Crop Services to implement this project.  
B.6. Exit strategy (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)


---
*Page 28*
---

B 
 GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL  V.3.0 | PAGE 22 
 
  
 The project will work at three levels: community level on planning and implementation systems as well as  at the 
Regional and National level regarding investments in weather forecast capability that will serve the Northern Sector, 
complementing investments in forecasting capability in the South of the country  as well as  establishing a national 
action plan for drought and flood hazard management .  The community level integrated ecosystem -based adaptation 
approach will inform the District level planning and bu dgeting systems. The project will build capacity and awareness 
of District planning officers to  scale up the project approach. C ombined with associated community training, extension 
services and awareness -raising, this will  increase opportunities for knowledge and technology exchange between 
communities, and thereby promote autonomous upscaling of these interventions.  
Fundamental aspects of the project’s sustainability are listed below.  
 
• The project will create capacity in District Authorities  to replicate and maintain climate adaptation activities after the 
initial investment period, through the integration of climate change adaptation into regional and district medium -
term development plans  as well as through the consolidation of a country -based financing mechanism to channel 
climate finance at the local level.   
• Beneficiary communities will receive extensive training on climate -resilient agricultural practices and the 
implementation of on -the-ground EbA interventions. In addition to building the adaptive capacity of these rural 
communities and allowing them to bec ome self -sufficient under climate change conditions,  an enabling 
environment will be created for the long -term maintenance and upscaling of proposed project activities. This will 
be further supported by the introduction of alternative climate -resilient liv elihoods that add value to agricultural 
products, thereby further promoting sustainability. This will include improved storage, processing equipment and 
access to finance.  
• A grassroots approach to building climate -resilience will ensure that activities are needs -based, locally appropriate 
and supported by local communities. An extensive community engagement process — including the development 
of Community Climate Action Plan s — will be carried out. Throughout this process women and youth will be 
targeted, particularly through the introduction of alternative climate -resilient livelihoods, to ensure their participation 
and support for project interventions. This will promote th e long -term commitment of community members after the 
project’s implementation period has elapsed.  
• The project will increase the access of smallholder farmers to financial resources by establishing FBOs that can 
access credit and insurance for farming and non -farming livelihood activities. In addition, the project will provide 
smallholder farmers in the FBOs, training in business and financial management. This enterprise approach will 
ensure that communities are equipped with the financial resources, business knowledge and financial capacity 
required to sust ain and upscale project interventions after the support phase.  The project will develop bankable 
deals for de -risking finance for access by smallholder farmers through the Agri3 Fund currently being established 
as a partnership between UNEP, Rabobank and t he Dutch Government.  
 
An extensive knowledge -sharing programme will support the project’s M&E activities, which will allow non -beneficiary 
communities to learn from the experiences of nearby target communities. In addition, annual knowledge -sharing 
conferences will function as a platform for discussions on the lessons learned and sharing of new ideas and research 
on EbA to ensure the sustainability of interventions under future climate change conditions. Subsequently, this 
knowledge base will be used as a resource to leverage ad ditional funds from public and private sector investors to 
maintain and replicate project interventions after the project’s funding period, while further strengthening cross -
sectoral collaboration.

C.3 Capacity building and technology development/transfer (max. 250 words, approximately 0.5 page)  
C.3.1 Does GCF funding finance capacity building 
activities?  Yes ☒    No ☐ 
C.3.2. Does GCF funding finance technology 
development/transfer?  Yes ☐    No ☒ 
The proposed project will build the technical and institutional capacity of regional and district government staff 
and local smallholder farming communities to implement and monitor climate change adaptation projects under 
Outputs 1  - 5 of the project . Output 1 will transition farmers to a proactive planning and management approach 
to climate hazards. Under Output 2, 3 and 4 of the project, capacity -building activities will focus on training 
extension officers on climate -resilient agriculture and EbA (Activity 3.1); beneficiary communities on: i) climate -
resilient agriculture, EbA and alternative climate -resilient livelihoods (Activity 3. 2); ii) basic business and 
financial management (Activity 3.2); and iii) processes for applying for credit and insurance from LFIs (Activity 
4.2). Capacity -building activities conducted under Output 3 will allow communities to become financially self -
sufficient, creating opportunities for further investments into climate -resilient livelihoods.  
 
 
 EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA  
This section refers to the performance of the project/programme against the investment criteria as set out in the 
GCF’s  Initial  Investment Framework .  
D.1. Impact potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page ) 
The proposed project will contribute to the GCF’s fund -level adaptation objective of increased climate -resilient 
sustainable development by reducing the vulnerability of smallholder farmers in northern Ghana through the 
implementation and adoption of clima te-resilient agriculture, ecosystem -based adaptation and alternative climate -
resilient livelihoods. The number of direct and indirect beneficiaries, disaggregated by gender, and the project’s 
contributions to the GCF’s outcome -level objectives and specific  results areas are described and quantified below42.   
 
Adaptation impact potential through increased resilience  
 
The proposed project will contribute to three GCF adaptation results areas (ARAs), namely: i) ARA1 — increased 
resilience of the most vulnerable people and communities; ii) ARA2 — increased health, well -being, food and water 
security; ARA3 - Infrastructure and built environment  and i v) ARA4 — increased resilience of ecosystems and 
ecosystem services. Specific contributions to these result areas are described in the following sub -sections.  
 
Direct beneficiaries of the proposed project will include the 691,125  rural crop farmers who will benefit from improved 
coverage by Early Warning Systems (Output 1), as well as 6920 farmers benefitting from  improved  climate -resilient 
agricultural practices  and 120,000 farm ers benefitting from agroforestry and restoration  (Output 3)  
 
Indirect beneficiaries comprises the population of the three target regions in northern Ghana — 2,861,674  people — 
who will benefit from the enhanced food and water security and improved resilience to climate hazards delivered by the 
proposed project  through the improvement of climate data and early warnings dissemination (Output 1). This 
information will be used to, inter alia , coordinate national -level drought and flood risk planning and responses, which 
will improve the climate -responsiveness of national planning in the future. For more information on the project’s outputs, 
see Section 13 of Annex 2: Feasibility Study.  
 
ARA1 – Increased resilience of the most vulnerable people and communities  
 
 
42 Detailed information on the assumptions and estimates of the impact potential of the proposed project are provided in the
