 Ghana’s early warning system will be enhanced by a range of interventions that will collectively facilitate access to 
improved information on floods and droughts (Activity 1.1), expand hydrometeorological and groundwater monitoring 
observation networks and  expand national radar coverage, particularly in underserved Northern region  which will 
significantly improve the accuracy of GMet’s numerical weather prediction model and  capacity to generate high skill 
forecast products (Activity 1.2) , build capacities  to equip personnel with advanced skills in drought and flood monitoring, 
assessment, and response, incorporating the latest technologies and best practices (activity 1.3) . as well as  
establishing a robust national framework for generating skill advisories and disseminating, inter alia , climate -related 
hazard management information to local communities (Activity 1.4).   These efforts will be supported by a national 
action plan for drought and flood hazard management (Activity 1.5). Although the interventions will be primarily 
designed to support direct beneficiaries (e.g., rural crop farmers), indirect beneficiaries —such as local businesses, 
families, and surrounding communities —will al so gain access to early warning data, which will enhance their ability to 
plan and reduce disaster risks, thus improving resilience.  
 
ARA2 – Increased health, well -being, food and water security  
 
Ghana’s early warning system  will be enhanced under Output 1 to facilitate proactive drought and flood management. 
Access to up -to-date climate information, facilitated by the mobile application (Activity 1.4), will benefit 691,125  rural 
crop farmers  (52% women and 48% men),  across the country  who will be empowered to effectively plan for and 
respond to drought and flood hazards, effectively reducing their food  insecurity. Proactive measures that farmers will 
be supported to undertake via this intervention include, inter alia : i) timing crop planting for optimum yields; ii) shifting 
to drought - and flood -resistant crops; and iii) relocation of agricultural efforts to more suitable sites that will minimise 
crop losses.  These proactive measures will reduce the impacts of extreme weather events on food production, leading 
to greater food security for rural households. Additionally, improved management of water resources, coupled with 
climate -resilient agricultural practices, will ensure more reliable w ater availability for both agricultural and household 
use, contributing to improved health and well -being by reducing the risks of food and water shortages during periods 
of climatic stress.  Furthermore, by reducing the risks of crop losses and supporting improved agricultural practices, the 
food security of broader rural populations, including those not directly targeted by the interventions, will also improve. 
Additionally, local ecosystems’ stability from proactive management of agricultural lands will c ontribute to improved 
water security in rural areas.  
 
Food security will also be improved  through activities under Output 2 by the introduction of climate -resilient agricultural 
interventions across northern Ghana , including measures such as crop rotation and intercropping, slash and mulching, 
conservation tillage, contour  ploughing, contour bunding, vegetative barriers, ridging, organic compositing and zai pits, 
cover cropping, introducing climate -resilient seed varieties, adapted planting calendar and dry season gardening . These 
strategies will  benefit 6,920 farms  (and 13, 840 hectares) , representing  an average of 2% population in the 8 Districts.    
 
Investments under Output 3 will improve the integrity of soils, supporting water infiltration to the aquifers, accumulation 
of nutrients and moisture in the soils and preventing soil erosion  which, coupled with heavy rainfall events, leads to 
flooding and loss of physical and natural assets.  
 
ARA3 - Infrastructure and built environment  
 
Investments under Output 2 and Output 3 will impro ve the resilience of the land and livelihoods of households to the 
impacts of extreme weather events on infrastructure and the built environment within the eight districts and 120 
beneficiary communities.   The planned investments in water storage tanks and small -scale water infrastructure under 
Output 2  in the 120 beneficiar y communities will be designed in order to withstand extreme weather events .  The 
restoration activities within the landscape will also serve to mitigate against loss to in frastructure and the built 
environment.  
 
ARA4 – Increased resilience of ecosystems and ecosystem services  
 
The site -level restoration efforts implemented under Output 3 — including agroforestry, small -scale communal fodder 
banks, riverbank restoration, flood risk reduction and fire management — are expected to improve the resilience of 
14,000 ha of vulnerable ecosystems and ecosystem services.   A further 120 beneficiary communities, comprising a 
total 120,000 people (assuming an average size of 1,000 people per community), will benefit from land restoration and 
agro-forestry on communal land.  
 
The Economic modelling carried out for the economic and financial assessment estimated that restoration of 14, 000 
ha of degraded land in strategic locations in four Districts would reduce flood risk by 46.7%.  The budget allows for 
restoration of 14,000 ha across the 8 Districts  (Output 3) .  Impacts against this indicator would be verifiable in longer

impacts.  
 
GCF Outcome -level enabling environment indicators  
The proposed project will be evaluated against several relevant GCF impact assessment factors, as specified in the 
GCF Integrated Results Management Framework . It is anticipated that the project will achieve two Outcome -level 
indicators,  described below43. 
 
• Core Indicator 5: Degree to which GCF investments contribute to strengthening institutional and regulatory 
frameworks for low emission climate -resilient development pathways in a country -driven manner; and  
• Core indicator 8: Degree to which GCF investments contribute to effective knowledge generation and learning 
processes, and use of good practices, methodologies and standards.  
 
Degree to which GCF investments contribute to strengthening institutional and regulatory frameworks for low emission 
climate -resilient development pathways in a country -driven manner .  
 
The project will build capacity and awareness of District planning officers to  scale up the project approach. C ombined 
with associated community training, extension services and awareness -raising, this will  increase opportunities for 
knowledge and technology exchange between communities and thereby promote autonomous upscaling of these 
interventions.  
At the field level, strengthened institutional development will be achieved by providing REMCs, DEMCs, as well as 
Regional EPA and Department of Agriculture offices with equipment required for effective and efficient delivery and 
monitoring of climate chan ge adaptation and agricultural extension services. Furthermore, the project will train  200 
extension officers (30%  of which  are women) in the target districts on climate -resilient agricultural processes, EbA and 
alternative climate -resilient livelihoods. K nowledge will be extended to smallholder farmers in target communities — 
and other communities not targeted by the project. In addition, , the utilization of grants for purposes that include both 
capacity building and TA aimed at adaptation mainstreaming on the one hand, and a country -based, progressively 
institutionalized local adaptation financing mechanism, create a holistic and integrated  approach to tackle the issue of 
climate finance deployment at the sub -national level and boosting of resleince build ing efforts at the community level .  
  
A monitoring programme will be designed to monitor project progress towards its targets and evaluate the socio -
economic impacts of the various climate change adaptation interventions being implemented. The monitoring 
programme will be designed in collaboration with relevant national research institutions, such as University for 
Development Studies, Kwame Nkrumah University of Science and Technology, Forest Research Institute of Ghana 
Soil Research Institute and Ghana M eteorological Agency. The monitoring programme will build an evidence base for 
the further implementation of climate change adaptation interventions in northern Ghana.  
 
Degree to which GCF investments contribute to effective knowledge generation and learning processes, and use of 
good practices, methodologies and standards .  
 
An awareness -raising campaign will be implemented in both beneficiary and non -beneficiary communities in northern 
Ghana. The campaign will increase awareness about appropriate climate change adaptation interventions and will 
promote the upscaling of these interventions outside of the project target sites. The awareness -raising campaign will 
be develop ed by a suitably qualified national organisation and will utilise locally -appropriate communication and 
knowledge dissemination mechanisms, such as video docum entaries and phone -in radio shows. All of the awareness -
raising activities included in the campaign will be appropriate to local circumstances, taking into account literacy levels 
and access to different types of media.  Various knowledge products detailing best practice and lessons learned through 
the project will be developed. These knowledge products will include inter alia : i) policy briefs; ii) climate change 
adaptation intervention implementation guidelines; iii) training manuals; and iv) reports. These knowledge products will 
then be disseminated to relevant stakeholders, including through the annual knowledge -sharing conferences (Activity 
5.2) and the Ghana Climate Cha nge Data Hub.

D.2. Paradigm shift potential ( max.  500 words, approximately  1 page ) 
The proposed project will catalyse a paradigm shift towards climate -resilient agriculture and natural resource -based 
livelihoods in northern Ghana. This will be achieved by facilitating the widespread adoption by smallholder farmers of 
climate -resilient ag ricultural practices, EbA approaches and alternative livelihoods. Details on how the project will 
contribute to GCF’s paradigm shift objective are presented below.  
 
Potential for scaling up and replication  
Decision -support services will be introduced under Output 1 to empower  farmers and planning officials to respond 
effectively to climate change -induced flood and drought hazards in a coordinated and strategic manner. Investments in 
weather forecast capability that will serve the Northern Sector will complement investments in forecasting capability in 
the South of the country  as well as  establishing a national action plan for drought and flood hazard management .  
Finally, a national framework for disseminating climate information to communities will be implemented to promote 
national and grassroots knowledge -sharing.  The plan will include a national coordination mechanism to promote 
collaboration between national and regional stakeholders. A national action plan for coordinating drought and flood 
hazard management in the agricultural sector will be delivered to ensure that EbA interventions can be replicated 
beyond the targeted areas.  
 
Regarding Output s 2 and 3, Within the eight project districts, there is an estimated 240,000 ha of farmland on which 
climate -resilient agriculture and livestock management can be practiced (representing 12 –66% of the district area)44. 
This represents a  potential 10 -fold increase in the  potential impact from this project. Regarding protection from fluvial 
floods, there are 8,700 ha of buffer zone protection concerning riverine forest protection and 9,200 ha of river buffer 
protection.  A monitoring and evaluation strategy will be implemented under Output 5 to enable best practices and 
lessons learned during EbA implementation to be documented and disseminated, further enabling future replication. 
The model can be replicated in 43 districts in the Northern Savannah zone  for drought and flood management .  
 
The Economic modelling carried out for the economic and financial assessment estimated that restoration of 14,000 
ha of degraded land in strategic locations in four Districts would reduce flood risk by 46.7% in the 4 Districts.  The 
budget allows for restoration of 14,000 ha across the 8 Districts  (Output 3) .   
 
The implementation of climate -resilient agricultural practices (Output 3 ) is expected to generate consistently higher 
incomes for smallholder farmers, allowing them to finance adaptation interventions once the project has finished. 
Farmer -based Organisations and their financial management capacity, and by linking to value chains and the 
Ghanaian financial sector for loans and insurance. A monitoring and evaluation strategy will be implemented under 
Output 4 to enable best practices and lessons learned durin g EbA implementation to be documented and 
disseminated, further enabling future replication.  
The knowledge generated will be used as a resource to leverage additional funds from public and private sector 
investors to maintain and replicate project interventions after the project’s funding period, while further strengthening 
cross -sectoral collaboration ( Output 5 ). 
 
Potential for knowledge sharing and learning  
A monitoring and economic valuation programme will be designed to monitor project progress towards its targets and 
evaluate the socio -economic impacts of the various climate change adaptation interventions being implemented. The 
monitoring programme will b e designed in collaboration with relevant national research institutions, such as University 
for Development Studies, Kwame Nkrumah University of Science and Technology, Forest Research Institute of Ghana 
Soil Research Institute and Ghana Meteorological Ag ency. A climate change intervention monitor will be placed in each 
targeted community to collect relevant information stipulated in the monitoring programme. Monitoring and evaluation 
will focus primarily on inter alia : i) the achievement of project targets (see Section E); ii) socio -economic benefits of 
project interventions; iii) changes in crop productivity under different climate -resilient agriculture and EbA scenarios; iv) 
socio -economic benefits and uptake of alte rnative climate -resilient livelihoods;  and v) environmental co -benefits of

D.3. Sustainable development ( max. 500 words, approximately 1 page ) 
The proposed project will use GCF funding to contribute towards the achievement of 13 of the 17 UN Sustainable 
Development Goals (SDGs)46 in northern Ghana, including: 1) No Poverty; 2) Zero Hunger; 3) Good Health and Well -
being; 5) Gender Equality; 6) Clean Water and Sanitation; 8) Decent Work and Economic Growth; 9) Industry, 
Innovation and Infrastructure; 10) Reduced Inequalities; 11) Su stainable Cities and Communities; 12) Responsible  
Consumption and Production; 13) Climate Action; 15) Life on Land; and 17) Partnerships for the Goals. The 
sustainability of contributions to the SDGs will be ensured through a multifaceted exit strategy, as described in Section 
B.6. Furthermore, the proje ct will advance the work of the UN Country Team (UNCT) in Ghana by contributing to three 
of the results areas, namely : i) Inclusive, Resilient and Sustainable Economic Growth and Transformation, ii) 
Accessib le, Equitable, Inclusive, Sustainable Quality Basic Social Services and iii) Durable Peace and Security in 
Ghana and the Subregion (UNSDP, 2023 -2025).  The UNSDP — which is a strategic partnership agreement between 
the UN and the GoG — reflects Ghana’s national goals and its commitments to global development initiatives and sets 
out the UN system’s collective contributions to help the Government and othe r stakeholders achieve these goals.  
  
In addition, the following co -benefits are expected from project interventions:  
• Reforestation through an EbA approach will be achieved through the planting of climate -resilient indigenous tree 
species and natural regeneration47 in previously degraded areas. The reforested areas will provide canopy cover, 
which will reduce soil erosion and capture runoff. Forest restoration through EbA will yield considerable co -benefits 
for local communities such as: i) providing NTFPs; ii) impr oving the dry -season flow of rivers; iii) increasing the 
recharging of aquifers; and iv) reducing community exposure to climate -related environmental hazards. These co -
benefits will  enhance the resilience of local communities to climate change while also diversifying local livelihoods.  
• The project activities are expected to sequester approximately 1.2 million tCO 2e over the lifespan of the project.  
• The proposed project will benefit northern Ghana’s most vulnerable people and least developed regions. This will 
be particularly important in coming decades as the GoG works to attain high -income status, as well as to being 
classified as a newly industrial ised country, by reversing the development divergence between the northern and 
southern regions of the country. The agricultural production of ~142,000 households in northern Ghana will be 
secured against the adverse effects of climate change, which will l ead to increased income generation and growth 
of the agro -based rural economy. Even at  suboptimal levels of production, the agricultural sector accounted for 
~20% of Ghana’s GDP in 201748. Increases in agricultural productivity across a large proportion of northern 
Ghanaian farms as a result of climate -resilient agriculture  and ecosystem -based adaptation interventions have the 
potential to increase the country’s GDP considerably and contribute to economic development. In addition, by 
increasing the resilience of post -harvest storage and processing of agricultural products, th ere will be a: i) decrease 
in wastage and loss of agricultural produce; ii) diversification in products that may be sold, which will include 
products that may be sold nationally and internationally; and iii) decrease in food shortages, as produce can be 
stored for use during the dry season.  
 
At least 50% of project beneficiaries are women. EbA interventions will be focussed on the empowerment of women, 
which is essential to increase their climate -resilience — as well as that of their communities. Shifts in gender roles 
and increases in women’s  contributions to household incomes have been shown to lead to greater respect for and 
recognition of women in Ghana49. Women will be targeted particularly for the implementation of alternative climate -
resilient livelihoods — which will  include value -addition activities such as shea butter production, small ruminant 
rearing, beekeeping and soap making.  Lastly, women and the youth will be considered and  included in the 
dissemination of early warning climate information.

46 http://www.un.org/sustainabledevelopment/sustainable -development -goals/

 GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V. 3.0 | PAGE 31 
   
 The GoG acknowledges the urgency of adapting to climate change in northern Ghana. The Climate Change 
Vulnerability (CCV) index50 calculated in the Feasibility Study51 confirms the GoGs concerns and indicates that the 
Upper West Region (CCV=0.61) is the most vulnerable in Ghana, followed by the North  East (CCV=0.26) and Upper 
East (CCV=0.26) Regions.  Northern Ghana is particularly at risk because of the substantial development gap between 
the north and the south of the country. This has isolated northern Ghana from the socioeconomic development that has  
been prevalent in the south. As a result, there has been financial instability and a rise in poverty in the north52. 
Smallholder farmers in northern Ghana — who rely heavily on rainfed agriculture and natural ecosystems for their 
livelihoods — are particularly vulnerable to the effects of climate change.  
 
The GOG is currently unable to fund adaptation projects that can significantly increase the climate resilience of the 
agricultural sector in northern Ghana without external assistance. This is because of the government’s limited fiscal 
capacity caused by, among others, a recent slow -down in economic growth, high inflation, currency depreciation and 
high domestic financing costs53. Indeed, the GOG has indicated that it currently has the fiscal capacity to provide ~6%  
of the funds required to implement the country’s INDCs54. Debt -to-GDP ratio was 64% in 2019. The lack of public 
financing for climate change adaptation has been exacerbated by the limited availability of alternative sources of funding 
from local capital markets. Private financial institutions have been unwilling to provide loan finance to individual 
smallhold er farmers in northern Ghana because of their general lack of collateral and high default risk55. Instead, the 
financial institutions have exclusively adopted joint liability lending structures restricted to servicing only registered 
FBOs56. Consequently, smallholder farmers in northern Ghana — many of whom are not organised into registered 
FBOs — have limited access to local capital markets from which they can obtain loans to finance adaptation 
interventions. The proposed project will contribu te to the financing of climate change adaptation in northern Ghana by: 
i) providing funding for the implementation of climate -resilient agriculture and EbA interventions in target districts; and 
ii) catalysing future private sector funding by organising sm allholder farmers into registered FBOs that can access loans 
from LFIs to finance adaptation interventions, and by increasing their access to international blended finance.  
 
In addition to financial support, the GoG requires strengthening and capacity building of regional and district institutions 
to enable them to manage climate change adaptation projects and integrate climate change adaptation into 
development planning. Presently, most district -level institutions in Ghana have limited capacity to manage and 
coordinate climate change adaptation projects. There is also limited integration of climate change adaptation into 
medium -term development plans in most regions and di stricts of Ghana. The proposed project will strengthen the 
technical capacity of regional and district institutions in northern Ghana to implement and monitor climate change 
adaptation projects by: i) training DEMC members in target districts on managing and c oordinating climate change 
adaptation projects; and ii) facilitating the integration of climate change adaptation into regional and district medium -
term development plans.  
D.5. Country ownership  (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page ) 
The Government of Ghana (GoG) recognises the current and future threats of climate change and has prioritised 
adaptation in the country’s national development strategies. In this regard, the GoG has adopted a National Climate 
Change Adaptation Strategy (NCCAS,  2010 -2020)57 that is designed to facilitate the protection of Ghana’s current and 
future development from climate change impacts by strengthening its adaptive capacity and building the resilience of 
society and ecosystems. The NCCAS outlines Adaptation objectives in nine development sectors, including: i) 
livelihoods; ii) energy; iii) agriculture; iv) health; v) early warning; vi) fisheries management; vii) land use; and viii) w ater. 
The implementation of the NCCAS will form part of the decent ralised planning and implementation system. Ministries, 
Departments and Agencies ( MDAs) at the national level are responsible for policy, planning, monitoring and evaluation 
of development programmes, with MESTI being the supervisory agency. It  aims to enhance the adaptive capacity and 
resilience to climate change in Ghana through: i) improving society’s awareness of and preparedness for future climate

 change; ii) mainstreaming of climate change into national development to reduce climate risks; iii) increasing the 
robustness of infrastructure development and long -term investments; iv) increasing the flexibility and resilience of 
vulnerable ecological an d social systems to enhance their adaptive capacity; and v) fostering competitiveness and 
promoting technological innovation.  The proposed project contributes directly to the NCCAS’s objectives of: i) improving 
awareness and preparedness to future climate change; ii) mainstreaming climate change adaptation into development 
planning; and iii) increasing the resilience of communities and ecosystems to climate change.  
 
In addition to the NCCAS, there are several policies and plans that aim to increase climate change adaptation and 
resilience  in Ghana, including the: i) National Climate Change Policy (NCCP, 2015 –2020)58; ii) Nationally Determined 
Contributions (NDC, 2020 -2030)59; iii) National Climate -resilient Agriculture and Food Security Action Plan (NCAFSAP, 
2016 -2020); and iv) National Adaptation Planning Readiness Proposal (NAP Readiness Proposal, 2020 –2024). The 
NCCP (2015 –2020) has five main policy sectors which include: i) Agriculture and Food Security; ii) Disaster 
Preparedness and Response; iii) Natural Resource Management; iv) Equitable Social Development; and iv) Energy, 
Industrial and Infrastructural Development. These policy areas have been subdivided into ten progr amme areas that 
address the critical issues of climate change in Ghana. The ten programme areas will include inter alia : 
• developing climate -resilient agriculture and food security systems;  
• building climate -resilient infrastructure;  
• increasing the resilience of vulnerable communities to climate -related risks;  
• improving management and resilience of ecosystems;  
• minimising impacts of climate change on access to water; and  
• addressing gender issues in climate change.  
 
The proposed project contributes to four of the NCCP’s ten programme areas: i) developing climate -resilient agriculture 
and food security systems; ii) increasing resilience of vulnerable communities to climate -related risks; iii) minimising 
impacts of clim ate change on access to water and sanitation; and iv) addressing gender issues in climate change; 
addressing climate change and migration.  
 
The 2018 NDC agriculture chapter is centred around three main agricultural priorities: conservation agriculture, climate 
smart technologies to increase fisheries and livestock productivity; post -harvest management and food processing. 
Complementary actions  on water conservation and irrigation development, agroforestry are highlighted as needing 
government attention. The project aligns with four of the seven priority adaptation actions in the NDC, specifically: i) 
agriculture resilience building in climate -vulnerable landscapes; ii) value addition -based utilisation of forest resources; 
iii) integrated water resources management; and iv) increasing the resilience of women and vulnerable people. Thirdly, 
the proposed project contributes to six of the eight action areas in the NCAFSAP, viz.: i) institutional capacity building 
for research and de velopment; ii) development a nd promotion of climate -resilient cropping systems; iii) adaptation of 
livestock production systems; iv) water conservation and irrigation systems; v) post -harvest management; and vi) risk 
transfer and alternative livelihoods. Lastly, the project will comp lement the  following three priorities in the NAP 
Readiness Proposal: i) training of government officials on climate risk analysis; ii)  development of adaptation plans ; 
and ii) mainstreaming of climate change adaptation into medium -term development plans.   
 
The proposed project is fully country -driven as it was prioritised by the National Designated Authority (NDA) in June 
2016 using a transparent prioritisation tool developed under the GCF Readiness Programme. It will help to achieve 
adaptation priorities in the agricultural sector as well as other wider objectives regarding disaster risk reduction, water 
resource management and ecosystem management in line with various policies and strategies in line with the 
provisions of the  Ghana Strategic Investment Fram ework for Sustainable Land Management (GSIF 2011 -2025). GSFI 
objectives are to: i) mainstream and upscale sustainable land -management decisions; ii) secure ecosystem services; 
and iii) improve rural livelihoods in Ghana. Activities under the auspices of GSIF will be designed to reverse land 
degradation to sustain high agricultural pro duction and ensure food security, whilst at the same time maintaining 
ecosystem integrity.

 In addition, extensive consultations with national, regional, district and community level stakeholders (including local 
government, private sector and civil society representatives, women and other vulnerable groups) were conducted in -
country by the GoG during the development of the proposal (see Annex 7 h). It is important to note that while these 
consultations were extensive, there were challenges in identifying and engaging with Indigenous Peoples present on 
the project landscape. This gap has been identified and will be addressed during the inception period and through the 
operationalization of an Indigenous Peoples Planning Framework (Annex 6C). The Environmental Protection Agency 
(EPA) of the Ministry of Environment, Science, Technology and Innovation (MESTI) will serve as the Executing Entity 
(EE) of the project in close collaboration with the Directorate of Crop Services of the Ministry of Food  and Agriculture 
(MoFA) , the Ghana Meteorological Agency (GM et) and the Ministry of Local Government, Chieftaincy and Religious 
Affairs (MLGCRA) . The EPA has a proven track record as an EE in Ghana, having been instrumental in the 
implementation of large -scale environmental programmes including, among others,  the GEMP and SLWMP (see 
Section B.4). UNEP — which has extensive experience in the design and implementation of initiatives that promote 
EbA — was nominated by the NDA in December 2016 to be the accredited entity for the project.  
D.6. Efficiency and effectiveness  (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page ) 
The inclusion of GCF funds in the proposed project will overcome the financial barriers to building the climate resilience 
of vulnerable smallholder farming communities in northern Ghana. As described in Section B.5, the GoG lacks the 
financial capacity to  initiate climate change adaptation projects without external financial support. Furthermore, the 
focus of the project on the most vulnerable communities in Ghana limits the potential for private investment into climate 
change adaptation initiatives. Howev er, with GCF support and using the cost -effective approach of EbA, the GoG will 
be able to increase the climate -resilience of the agro -based rural economy in northern Ghana effectively. There is a 
growing body of scientific evidence demonstrating the cost -effectiveness of past initiatives that have included an EbA 
approach to climate change adaptation60;61. 
A financial analysis was carried out (Annex 3a and b) to assess potential financial returns of some of the proposed 
project interventions. Many of the project activities are centred on institutional and technical capacity building, making 
them of a public goods nature. These activities will, therefore, not yield a direct return on investment. The financial 
analysis, therefore, focuses on those activities that involve on -the-ground interventions to improve the climate resilience 
of agriculture, land manageme nt and rural livelihoods. A cost -benefit  study was conducted based on cost estimations 
for the implementation of interventions and the project’s predicted outputs (Annex 3b). The cost estimates were based 
on current market values. A discount rate was then applied to determine the net present valu e (NPV) of each 
intervention. The financial analysis demonstrated positive NPVs for proposed project interventions such as: i) maize -
soybean crop rotation (NPV = US$563); ii) Cassia -maize intercropping (NPV = US$465 ); iii) mango -maize intercropping 
(NPV = US$463); and iv) Shea nut processing (NPV = US$4 -US$84 ). An additional analysis was conducted to 
determine the discounted Benefit to Cost Ratio (BCR) of both NBS and hybrid interventions for the period 2024 –2050 
(Annex 3a). This assessment revealed that both interventions are economically viable, with an estima ted BCR ranging 
between 2.09 and 3.44 for Lambussie, 2.19 and 3.78 for Lawra, 2.21 and 3.85 for Jirapa and 2.74 and 4.32 for WA 
West. The Internal Rate of Retu rn (IRR) ranges from 17 –38% across all locations ( Table 2, elaborated in Annex 3).

The design of proposed project interventions has drawn substantially from the best practices and lessons learned from 
a range of local and international development projects and studies. Additionally, stakeholder consultations with 
representatives from regional - and district -level government departments, as well as communities from the t arget 
districts, have provided insights on the local appropriateness of international best practices. The best practices and 
lessons learned include the: i)  Foundational Support Initiative which promotes community engagement; ii) use of 
Regional, District and Community Environmental Management Committees; iii)  the use of EbA as a framework to 
integrate sustainable land management and climate change adaptation interventions; iv) importance of integrating 
gender considerations into project interventions; and v) promotion of autonomous upscaling of project interven tions 
through knowledge sharing between beneficiary and neighbouring communities.  This will include knowledge sharing 
between pastoral and sedentary communities as possible, as many rural communities increasing ly practise mixed 
livelihood strategies  (which often includes a combination of traditional  pastoral practices  and local sedentary farming  
practic es or petty trading ). These knowledge sharing processes will be facilitated through an Indigenous Peoples Plan, 
given that pastoralists in Ghana are predominantly constituted of Indigenous groups such as the Fulani.  These lessons 
learned and best practices are discussed in detail in Section 10 of Annex 2, Feasibility Study. The project will also draw 
from the substantial experience of UNEP to maximise the benefits gained from past experiences and best practices. 
The development of a Gender Action Plan and an Environmental and S ocial Management Framework — guided by 
UNEP’s internal safeguards — will ensure that interventions are in line with GCF requirements and safeguards.

E.6. Project/programme activities and deliverables   
 
Activities  Description  Sub-activities  Deliverables  
Activity 1.1: Implement a new  
early warning data information 
and management system to 
provide access to improved 
data sources and new 
datasets  on floods and 
droughts . A new data and information management 
system, combined with an enhanced service 
delivery platform for hydrometeorological 
services (Activity 2.2), will facilitate the cross -
sectoral integration of up -to-date climate 
information and early warnings to enable  
proactive as opposed to reactive management. 
Easier access to improve data sources and 
new datasets facilitated by the improve early 
warning system will address the barriers 
highlighted by stakeholders. This data 
information system will introduce innovati ve 
modelling approaches, based on remote 
sensing data, and service delivery platforms. 
Additionally, with improved access to quality 
climate data information, farmers will be more 
actively involved in planning processes 
pertaining to flood and drought mana gement. 
Proactive measures that farmers will be 
supported to undertake via this intervention 
include, inter alia: i) timing crop planting for 
optimum yields; ii) shifting to drought -resistant 
crops; and iii) relocation of agricultural efforts 
to more suita ble sites that will minimise crop 
losses.  1.1.1    Implementing science -based methods for merging 
the existing Hydromet information with satellite -based 
information for improved accuracy of the data, and 
producing new seasons forecast within Ghana  as a web -
based portal.  
 
1.1.2 Development, validation and implementation of a 
web-based service delivery and decision -support (DSS) 
platform for weather and hydrological early warning 
services to be used by national and local agencies, 
including water resource allocation models, r isk 
knowledge services, drought and flood hazard monitoring 
and EWS.

implemented

Activity 1.5: Implement 
national action plan for 
coordinating drought and flood 
hazard management in the 
agricultural sector.  The existing National Drought Management 
Plan, delineating roles and responsibilities for 
key institutions, is set to be put into action. 
This implementation will activate the national 
coordination mechanism designed for 
managing flood and drought -related hazards. 
The plan provides a structured framework for 
coordinating efforts across various entities, 
streamlining responsibilities to enhance the 
efficiency of drought management. By 
executing this plan, the nation aims to foster 
collaboration among key ins titutions, ensuring 
a unified and organized approach to 
addressing the challenges posed by drought. 1.5.1  Review and provide recommendations for 
strengthening the existing strategic framework for 
drought - and flood -related hazard management, and 
develop a national coordination mechanism for 
responding to drought - and flood related hazards in the 
agricult ural sector,  
 
1.5.2 Produce an action plan for integration of climate 
information into policy and decision -making processes for 
drought and flood management, including actions to 
clearly define responsibilities for long term planning and 
proactive measures, and initiate implementation of the 
action plan.

Activity 2.1: Train extension 
officers on climate -resilient 
agricultural practices EbA and 
alternative climate -resilient 
livelihoods.  The project will train extension officers within 
targeted districts on: i) climate -resilient 
agriculture, EbA and alternative climate -
resilient livelihoods ii) development of 
Community Climate Action Plans (CCAPs); iii) 
the implementation of a dynamic menu  of 
climate change adaptation to northern Ghana. 
Training district extension officers to plan and 
implement climate change adaptation 
interventions will ensure that the officers are 
prepared to facilitate the implementation of 
climate change adaptation int erventions in 
target communities. Training programmes will 
be designed and conducted by appropriate 
national departments within the GoG, 
particularly the EPA, Department of Agriculture 
and Extension Services Division. The training 
will build on existing ex tension service 
manuals and guidelines and will include both 
theoretical and practical components. One 
training workshop per year will be conducted in 
each of the eight target districts for the 
duration of the project to ensure that extension 2.1.1: Develop training materials and guidelines for

Activity 2.2: Train beneficiary 
communities in northern 
Ghana on climate -resilient 
agricultural practices and EbA 
interventions.  The project will train beneficiary communities 
in northern Ghana on CRA, EbA and 
alternative climate -resilient livelihoods. Firstly, 
sensitisation workshops will be held in the first 
year that the project provides support to a 
community. District, Regional  and National 
government staff will facilitate the community 
sensitisation workshops.  Following this, staff 
from relevant departments of the GoG71 and 
extension officers will train beneficiary 
communities on climate change and the 
climate change impact pathways most relevant 
to them as smallholder farmers. Extension 
officers will then introduce the menu of climate 
change adaptation interventions to 
communities. Targeted communities will then 
be trained to implement climate change 
adaptation interventions identified within the 
menu of interventions. In addition, extension 
officers will organise farmers into special 
Common Interest Groups (CIGs) and pro vide 
targeted training on specific adaptation 
technologies from the menu of climate change 
adaptation interventions. Finally, 
demonstration sites will be established by 
extension officers with willing community 
members to demonstrate various climate 
change  adaptation interventions that can be 
implemented at the household level.   2.2.1: Organise project sensitisation workshops in each of 
the targeted communities.  
 
2.2.2: Develop training materials for smallholder farmers 
on climate change adaptation, CRA and EbA.  
 
2.2.3: Develop a training scorecard to measure the 
effectiveness of the training delivered to smallholder 
farmers.  
2.2.4: Deliver training to targeted communities on: i) 
climate change impacts; ii) use of DSS tools and 
services; and iii) menu of climate change adaptation 
interventions.  
 
2.2.5: Establish CIGs and deliver targeted training on 
specific climate change adaptation interventions.  
 
2.2.6: Establish five demonstration sites in each targeted 
community.

liability  agricultural loans and insurance 
identified during a survey of LFIs conducted for 
the AFAWA programme.  
 
The project will provide support to members of 
the established FBOs to register their 
organisations with MoFA (Sub -activity 4.1.1).

applying for credit and insurance from LFIs. 
Extension officers will deliver this training during 
the first year that each beneficiary community 
receives support to ensure that the FBOs meet 
the eligib ility criteria set by LFIs for joint liability 
lending.  
 
The project will train established FBOs to set up 
Village Savings and Loans Associations 
(VSLAs, Sub -activity 4.1.2). The purpose of 
establishing VSLAs is twofold. Initially, the 
VSLAs will provide simple savings and loan 
facilities to these communities wh o currently do 
not have access to formal financial services. 
Secondly, operating the VSLAs will enable 
them to maintain up -to-date financial records 
which will enable the FBOs to meet the eligibility 
requirements of LFIs for joint liability loans and 
insur ance.

E.7. Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)  
A full time Monitoring Officer will be employed to conduct and coordinate monitoring and evaluation (M&E) activities for the project. The Monitoring Officer will 
design and operationalise a performance monitoring framework to track the project’s progress t owards achieving its targets. This will include: i) measuring 
performance against the project indicators (Sections E.3 –5) to evaluate the progress of the project; ii) reporting the project’s performance to the PSC and PMU; 
and iii) providing technical supp ort to the PC. Additionally, the Monitoring Officer will be responsible for overseeing and monitoring the application of gend er-
disaggregated indicators and ensuring that all gender targets are met. At particular milestones throughout the project — includi ng annual performance reports, 
interim evaluations and at project conclusion — the project team will undertake evidence -gathering exercises to verify the progress. These internal results verification 
reports will be further validated by evaluations  at the mid-term and end of the project implementation, as described below. The costs for the mid -term and terminal 
evaluation will be covered by UNEP .  During the  Terminal Evaluation at the end of the project , the evaluation team will validate a sample of the data collected 
through the  monitoring framework .  
 
UNEP will be responsible for managing  a Mid-Term Review (MTR)  at the mid -point of  the project’s duration.  The Task Manager will oversee the process of hiring 
an external consultant to carry out the  MTR, which  will provide an assessment of project performance at the project’s mid -point.  This will  be a formative exercise 
and will  include analysing whether the project is on track, what problems and challenges the project is encountering, and which correc tive actions are required so 
that the project can achieve  its intended outcomes by project completion in the most efficient and sustainable way. The Project  Steering  Committee  will participate 
in the MTR process and develop a management response to the review’s recommendations along with an implementation plan.  It is the responsibility of the UNEP 
Task Manager to monitor whether the agreed recommendations are being implemented  during the remainder of the project’s operational life.  The Independent 
Evaluation Office of UNEP will provide an assessment of the qualit y of the Mid -Term Review report.   
 
An independent Terminal Evaluation (TE) will take place  once the project has reached operational completion. The Evaluation Office of UNEP will be responsible 
for the TE, which is a summative evaluation,  and will liaise with the UNEP Task Manager  and relevant stakeholders  throughout the process. An independent


---
*Page 70*
---

E 
 GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V. 3.0 | PAGE 64 
  
  
  
 
 assessment of project performance  against standard evaluation criteria  (e.g. strategic  relevance, effectiveness,  efficiency, likelihood of impact and sustainability) 
will be made based on documentary evidence, stakeholder interviews and, in most cases, a field mission.  Each evaluation criterion  will be  rated  using a three -point 
rating scheme  and a weighted average will be determined to provide an overall performance rating for the  project as a whole . Where there are any differences in 
ratings  between the ev aluation team and the Evaluation Office  a final determination will be made by the Evaluation Office  when the  evaluation  report is finalised.   
 
The draft TE report will be sent to project stakeholders  during a  commenting process managed by the Evaluation  Office. Formal comments on the report will be 
shared by the Evaluation  Office  in an open and transparent manner. This evaluation report will be publicly disclosed and will be followed by a recommendation  
compliance process.   
  
The costs for results monitoring and performance evaluation  are included in the ‘Detailed Budget’ sheet of Annex 4  under budget lines 284–285.  
 
The EE will submit semi -annual progress reports and quarterly financial statements to the PMU and the PMU will consolidate the reports and submit the m to UNEP 
as the AE. In turn, UNEP will submit annual performance reports and semi -annual financial reports  to GCF. The detailed reporting timelines are as follows:  
 
Under the PCAs, the EE will be required to report to UNEP as follows:  
a. Progress reports: by 30 July for January to June;  
b. Quarterly financial reports by 15 January, 15 April, 15 July, and 15 October;  
c. Annual audited statements by 30 April;  
d. Final report: within 12 months of Programme completion.  
 
UNEP (AE) reports to the GCF:  
a. Annual Performance Reports by 1 March;  
b. Semi -annual Financial Information by 1 March and 30 Sept;  
c. Mid-Term Assessment report: halfway through Programme;  
d. Final APR: within 6 months of Programme completion;  
e. Terminal Evaluation report: within 12 months of Programme completion.  
 
Monitoring will also be undertaken by the AE through at least two Task Manager supervision visits and field missions  per year  to track implementation progress and 
challenges and strategically plan the way forward.

 RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT  
F.1.  Risk factors and mitigations measures (max. 3 pages)   
AML/CFT and Prohibited Practices compliance were assessed in the UNEP capacity assessment of EPA. 
UNEP noted that there are no UN sanctions or embargoes listed against EPA or its Management team, 
and there appears to be no evidence of engagement in prohibited practices by EPA.  
 
Selected Risk Factor 1   
Category  Probability  Impact  
Governance  Medium  Medium  
Description  
Secure land tenure is critical for investment in climate change adaptations. If tenure is insecure, farmers face lower 
expected returns due to the risk of eviction, which undermines long -term planning and sustainability. Ambiguity in 
land-use rights can lead to disputes between  landowners, tenants and customary users, including pastoralists, 
resulting in tenure insecurity and reduced uptake of adaptation measures.  
 
To address this, the project will engage both landowners and land users, including pastoralist communities, and 
ensure that traditional and customary land use rights are respected  in line with the GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy. 
These rights, including access to water points and rivers, seasonal access to land grazing corridors, will be identified 
and safeguarded through meaningful consultations and securement of the Free, Prior and Informed Consent from 
Indigenous Peoples using  appropriate participatory pro cesses guided by the IPPF. Conflict -sensitive planning and 
local dispute resolution mechanisms will also be applied to reduce risks and ensure inclusive and sustainable 
implementation.  
 
 
Mitigation Measure(s)  
This risk will be managed through a participatory approach to planning and implementation of proposed project 
activities. Close engagement with local communities, GoG entities and traditional authorities in project execution will 
be a priority, while local officials’ knowledge and experience  in dealing with Ghana’s land tenure situation will be 
invaluable. Engagement with landowners will be a priority so that landowners are made aware of the benefits of the 
proposed CRA and EbA interventions and the resulting climate -resilience. Furthermore, the menu of climate change 
adaptation interventions will allow the landowner to decide which activities are implemented on their land. This will 
further promote the participation and ownership of landowners in the proposed project. Consequently, the autono mous 
uptake of project interventions by surrounding farmers and communities will be enhanced and sustainability will be 
strengthened. This mitigation measure is expected to lower the probability of the risk occurring to “Low”.  
Selected Risk Factor 2   
Category  Probability  Impact  
Other  Low High 
Description  
The occurrence of low community demand to implement or sustain project technologies. If the benefits of interventions 
are not locally relevant or the long -term benefits are not adequately explained to beneficiaries,  then communities may 
revert to previously used and unsustainable agricultural methods. As a result, environmental degradation will continue, 
which will lead to further losses of livelihoods and decreased food security.  
Mitigation Measure(s)  
Attention will be given to locally relevant benefits in the selection of activities to encourage community buy -in. The 
CCAPs will ensure community participation and allow the selection of the most locally appropriate interventions from 
the menu of climate change adaptation interventions. Such a participatory approach to planning and implementation 
will build local awareness on EbA and generate interest in project interventions. The autonomous upscaling of 
proposed project interventions will be promoted thro ugh carefully planned site selection and the facilitation of inter -


---
*Page 72*
---

E 
 GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V. 3.0 | PAGE 66 
   
 community meetings. M&E of project interventions will be implemented, which will allow for the adaptive management  
of interventions. This will ensure the long -term sustainability of proposed project interventions and promote their 
viability.  
Selected Risk Factor 3   
Category  Probability  Impact  
Other  Medium  High 
Description  
Local communities may revert to unsustainable harvesting and farming practices after the project. In particular, 
trees planted during forest restoration may be cut down for fuelwood or to make way for new fields.  
Mitigation Measure(s)  
This risk will be mitigated  by conducting awareness -raising campaigns in local communities about the specific 
economic benefits that restored climate -resilient forest ecosystems provide. Additionally, forests will be restored 
with tree species that offer higher financial returns to l ocal communities than the sale of fuelwood. Fast -growing, 
climate -resilient species that provide valuable NTFPs will be included in plantings to demonstrate the benefits of 
reforestation to communities before the end of the project. These mitigat ion measures will adjust the probability of 
this risk occurring to “Low”.  
Selected Risk Factor 4  
Category  Probability  Impact  
Other  Low High 
Description  
The use of pesticides in interventions results in ecosystem damage through the secondary poisoning of non -target 
bird and mammal species.  
Mitigation Measure(s)  
The proposed project will ensure that the pesticides used by smallholder farmers have minimal impacts on 
agroecosystems. Furthermore, extension officers will train smallholder farmers on the safe storage, use and disposal 
of pesticides, as well as informin g them about alternative non -toxic pest management techniques. Part Two of 
Ghana’s Environmental Protection Agency Act (1994)  makes provisions for the effective control and management of 
pesticides. This is aligned with the FAO’s International Code of Cond uct80 in terms of handling, storing, applying and 
disposing of pesticides. The control of pesticide use in Ghana is enforced by  EPA, which is supported by the 
Pesticides Technical Committee. Furthermore, the proposed project will use a proactive integrated pest management 
system (including inter alia  the use of pesticides and post -harvest management) to mitigate the impacts of pests on 
agricultural production and overall project success.  
Selected Risk Factor 5   
Category  Probability  Impact

and services is likely to be stimulated over time as the sup ply increases and as communities become more reliant on

 them. Additionally, the post -harvest processing and storage of these products will be offered in the menu of climate

the extension officers and regio nal/district branches. National level entities will provide the necessary technical 
backstopping. This mitigation measure will reduce the probability of the risk occurring to “Low”.

manual that clearly defines the policies and procedures to ensure consistency in financial operations and reporting; 
and i ii) periodic Value for Money Audits on the beneficiary agencies will be conducted by the Internal Audit of the EPA 
to ensure that project objectives are achieved. These mitigation measures will decrease the probability of the risk 
occurring to “Low”.

take place to inform and engage on project activities, and grievance redress mechanism will be established to ensure 
that any potential conflicts or issues are tacked immediately and addressed in a way that is agreeable to all parties 
involved.

 GCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS  
G.1.  Environmental and social risk assessment (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)   
The project has been designed to take potential environmental and social impacts of project activities into consideration, 
incorporating mitigating measures to reduce the likelihood and severity of unforeseen adverse effects. All project 
activities have be en evaluated against GCF Environmental and Social Safeguard (ESS) standards using UNEP’s 
Safeguards Risk Identification Form  (SRIF )81 as a screening tool to identify potential negative impacts. The SRIF  
screens the significance of environmental, social and economic risks against safeguard standards outlined in the 
UNEP’s Environmental and Social Sustainability Framework  (ESSF )82. The SRIF  Screening  Decision (Annex 6A)  
classified the project as moderate risk , with moderate risk factors associated with UNEP’s Safeguards Standard 1: 
Biodiversity, Ecosystems and Sustainable Natural Resource Management and Safeguards Standard 7: Indigenous 
Peoples .  
 
As the project is classified as moderate risk, an  Environmental and Social Management Framework (ESMF) (see Annex 
6B) has been develo ped. This ESMF provides explicit and systematic guidelines to identify environmental and social 
risks linked to project interventions and outlines the institutional and administrative pathways to address these risks. 
The ESMF identifies environmental and social risks and impacts that may be caused by the proposed project, including: 
i) introduction of harmful specie s; ii) increased use of agricultural chemicals; iii) increased demand for irrigation; iv)  
habitat conversion; v)  excessive fire suppression; and v) the adverse effects of on -farm earthworks. The framework 
provides mitigatory measures for avoiding, minimisi ng and mitigating the harmful risks and impacts such as: i) screening 
for invasive species; ii) adopting integrated pest and nutrient management approaches; iii) avoiding  large -scale or 
diesel pump irrigation; iv) preventing the conversion of natural habitats to cropland or plantation; and v)  supporting 
controlled early burning, rather than outright fire suppression.  
 
Additionally, during the project development period, social risks related to ethnic minorities, inclusive engagement 
processes and potential exacerbation of community conflict were identified. These social risks emerge through potential 
for conflict betwee n ethnic minorities  (including those categorised as Indigenous Peoples per the GCF IP Policy)  
practicing different and sometimes competitive livelihood strategies, namely sedentary farming and semi -
nomadic/transhumant pastoralism. The potential for these r isks to emerge are exacerbated by the project’s focus on 
sedentary livelihood strategies and  general trend of  underrepresentation of pastoral groups within government 
institutions and institutional processes . To address these risks, an Indigenous Peoples Planning Framework (IPPF) has 
been developed in accordance with the GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy.  The IPPF will be implemented in the first year 
of project implementation and will guide the identification of Indigenous Peoples, ensure FPIC is secured prior to 
activities affecting them, and enable  the development of site -specific Indigenous Peoples Plans (IPPs)  in consultation 
with the relevant Indigenous Peoples . These plans will ensure that potential adverse impacts are addressed, that 
Indigenous Peoples are equitably included in project processes, and that their collective rights , land use practices, and 
their traditional knowledge are respected and safeguarded .  
 
As the Executing Entity, the EPA will oversee the implementation/monitoring of the ESMF  and IPP and provide technical 
guidance and specialist advice on environmental and social issues to all stakeholders83. In addition, all potential delivery 
organisations — including private contractors — will be vetted by the EPA in terms of their environmental and social 
performance to ensure compliance with the ESMF. The EPA  — which is responsible for ensuring compliance with 
environmental impact assessment (EIA) procedures under the EPA Act 1994, Act 490 — has the experience and 
capacity to implement the ESMF.  
 
To guarantee effective project implementation and to address grievances that may arise as a result of project 
interventions,  Grievance Redress Mechanisms will be established in each district, detailing the complaints procedure 
and the redress mechanisms for eligible84 grievances (see Annex 6 b). All community members and stakeholders will 
be informed of the grievance redress mechanism, including how to register a complaint. At the community level, 
complaints will be presented to Climate Change Action Groups linked to the proposed project. These  groups will receive 
and record the charges, as well as issue an acknowledgement. Mediation bodies appropriate to the nature of the

 grievance — including community elders, Tindanas or chiefs — will be established that will endeavour to reach mutually 
acceptable solutions within 20 working days. All efforts will be made to address grievances at the community level. 
Should any complaints  arise that cannot be resolved at the first level of the Grievance Redress Mechanism , these will 
be referred to project staff, through Grievance Redress Committees at the district level. The Grievance Redress 
Committees will hold meetings with complainants  to identify a solution acceptable to all parties involved. The proposed 
redress actions will be communicated to complainants and if satisfactory, implemented promptly. A formal complaints 
register will be established by the EPA in which all concerns raise d through the grievance mechanism will be registered 
within 24 hours. The GRM is based on an existing GRM utilised by the EPA for the LoCAL project as is, therefore, well 
established and operating effectively.  In addition to the standard project level GRM,  the project will: i) include a parallel 
SEAH -specific survivor -centred process, which is detailed in the ESAMF; ii) ensure access to the UNEP SRM for any 
complainants; iii) ensure information is disseminated on how to access the GCF IRM (and notably the I ndigenous 
Peoples Specialist within the Green Climate Fund); and finally iv) develop an Indigenous Peoples -specific GRM through 
the operationalization of the IPPF to ensure the provision of a culturally appropriate process that respects the traditional 
authority structures of any potentially affected Indigenous Peoples.  
 
Extensive on -the-ground public consultations and stakeholder engagements were conducted during the design of the 
proposed project to identify the needs of the beneficiary communities and the potential social risks that the project 
may encounter85. As highlighted in section D.5, it was not possible to undertake comprehensive consultations with 
Indigenous Peoples present on the landscape who may be impacted by the project. These groups will be mapped 
and consulted (with FPIC secured where required) th rough the implementation of the IPPF.  These consultations will 
continue throughout the project lifespan  and in accordance with  an IPP in any landscape in which Indigenous 
Peoples are present . In addition, the inclusion of community members in the development and validation of 
Community Climate Action Plans (CCAPs) will facilitate the identification of community needs. The combination of 
community consultations, stakeholder engagements and the i nclusive development of CCAPs will ensure acceptance 
of project activities from the beneficiary communities.  
G.2.  Gender assessment and action plan (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)   
During project preparation, a gender assessment was conducted to understand the differential vulnerability to, and 
impact of, climate change on women and men in Ghana. From the findings of this assessment, and an analysis of the 
proposed project solution  and interventions , a gender action plan (GAP) was developed. The process to produce these 
components included: i) a desktop review of available literature; ii) gender equality studies conducted by research 
institutions and donor agencies; ii) details made available by the Government of Ghana; and iv) information obtained 
during extensive stakeholder and community consultations  in 2017 and 2024 .  
 
The assessment notes that gender inequalities in Ghana disproportionately affect the adaptive capacity of women to 
current and future climate change, as opposed to men. The effects of climate change on households and communities 
will ultimately be exacerba ted if the adaptive capacit ies of both men and women are not strengthened in parallel. 
Overlooking either gender will affect production and income within a household, having knock -on effects on families, 
children and the community as a whole. Although trad itional knowledge has established coping mechanisms within 
communities to the harsh climatic conditions experienced in northern Ghana, it is unlikely to adapt quickly enough to 
overcome the effects of future climate change.  
 
Women  — including divorcees and widows  — along with children and the elderly, are among the most vulnerable 
groups to the effects of climate change. This is largely because women and female -headed households have fewer 
resources to cope with reductions in food and water security, together with th e effects of extreme weather events such 
as droughts and floods. This is particularly pertinent in the target  communities, where people are often reliant on climate -
sensitive natural resources for their livelihoods. Severa l factors contribute to the vulnerability of women to climate 
change, including, inter alia , the effects of migration, water availability, limited access to climate information or early 
warnings, decreased agricultural yields and reliance on climate -sensitive livelihoods.  
 
Mainstreaming gender into development frameworks and project design — – including conceptualisation, 
implementation, monitoring and evaluation —  is therefore important to build the adaptive capacity of households  and 
communities and reduce the social impacts of climate change on communities. To mitigate against biasing against 
women as project beneficiaries during implementation and subsequently, the gender action plan outlines several 
 
85 The potential environmental and social risks that may arise during project implementation, as well as mitigation measure to 
reduce these risks, are presented in Annex 6: Environmental and Social Assessment and Management Framework. In 
addition, summaries of all stakeholder consultations conducted during project development, as well as the stakeholder 
engagement plan, are presented in Annex 7 h: Stakeholder Engagement Plan.

*Page 77*

F 
 GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V. 3.0 | PAGE 71 
   
 interventions to ensure the project does not unintentionally contribute to perpetuating or worsening gender equality and 
gender -based climate vulnerability in Ghana. The GAP seeks to promote gender equality by including activities which 
will increase women ’s representation and awareness, as well as ensuring that training programmes are gender -
responsive  and -inclusive. It includes clear targets, gender -responsive  design features and quantifiable performance 
indicators to promote women’s participation and be nefits from the project.  
 
The project interventions will address gender equality through several pathways, including:  
• promoting women’s inclusion in all project aspects, including training and awareness -raising opportunities;  
• incorporating gender considerations  into the development of community climate action plans, based on the specific 
gaps identified within each community;  
• integrating women into the institutional structures and involving them in the decision -making process;  
• pursuing representative participation in all consultations, workshops and validation events;  
• providing women with business and financial training;  
• raising awareness on the importance of equal opportunity for all people and the role women play in climate change 
adaptation across a community;  
• sharing lessons learned through project interventions to help create a paradigm shift in the approach to gender 
equality in climate change adaptation projects; and  
• designing and implementing gender -responsive  training that considers the different learning methods and training 
accessibility of men and women.  
• working closely with local community leaders, civil society organisations and pastoralist associations to identify and 
invite pastoralist representatives to participate in stakeholder consultations, workshops, and validation events.  
• ensuring diversity within these groups, including equitable participation of women pastoralists, to capture a wide 
range of experiences and perspectives.  
• ensur ing that logistical and linguistic barriers to participation are addressed, enabling inclusive and effective 
engagement with pastoralist communities throughout the project cycle.  
 
Gender -specific indicators defined in the GAP will be used to quantitatively assess the gender -specific benefits of 
project interventions on an ongoing basis. These indicators will ensure that the monitoring and evaluation process is 
gender -sensitive and t hat project interventions are effectively promoting gender equality in climate change adaptation. 
The results of these assessments will be incorporated into the annual Project Implementation Reports, Mid -term Report 
and Terminal Evaluation.  The full Gender  Assessment and project -level Gender Action Plan are included as Annex 8.  
G.3.  Financial management and procurement (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)  
The financial management and procurement within the project will be guided by UN financial regulations, rules and 
practices, as well as UNEP’s programme manual. The financial rules of UNEP, which follow International Public Sector 
Accounting Standards (IPSAS), are promulgated under the Financial Regulations and Rules of the UN. Within this 
context, funding -allocation mechanisms are managed as per UN rules and procedures, including eligibility criteria, 
proposal evaluation pro cesses, quality assurance and control, project monitoring and supervision. UNEP is audited 
annually by the UN Board of Auditors and has established dedicated trust funds for Green Climate Fund (GCF) 
resources.  
 
The funding of the project will be conducted  through a distinct grant within the Trust Fund, with the project itself being 
set up in the UNEP Enterprise Resource Planning (ERP) as a Work Breakdown Structure (WBS), which is itself further 
broken down into Work Breakdown Structure Elements (WBSEs), org anised by output and outcome. The grant of the 
project will be linked to the WBSEs to fund the activities contributing to the delivery of specific outputs — as per the 
disbursement schedule detailed in the term sheet. The fee income will be managed independently and separately of 
the GCF project grant funds, through a dedicated grant . In line with UNEP procedures, the project will appoint a 
Financial Officer within the PMU who will be responsible for monitoring, reporting on and approving requests for funds 
on a quarterly basis. Reports to summarise the disbursement and projected dema nds for project funding will be 
prepared and submitted to a UNEP Task Manager who will undertake project supervision, in line with r eporting 
standards and methodologies applied in past projects, such as those implemented using GEF modalities. The UN 
financial regulations and rules require the segregation of duties, and safeguards to ensure compliance with UN financial 
rules and regulat ions. All procurement will be undertaken in line with UN procurement regulations, rules and policies. 
UNEP’s modalities for project implementation results in funds being transferred in tranches to the EE once the EPA 
has satisfied the conditions that are d efined under the legal/cooperation instrument (Project Cooperation Agreement; 
PCA and Internal Cooperation Agreement; ICA) to be signed between UNEP Climate Change Adaptation Unit, 
Ecosystems Division and the EPA. The PCA will include specific obligations for EPA on financial management and 
reporting and will require periodic reporting from EPA to follow international financial and auditing standards. The PCA


---
*Page 78*
---

F 
 GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V. 3.0 | PAGE 72 
   
 specifically requires the audit to be undertaken by a recognised firm of certified public accountants or, for governments, 
by a government auditor. This auditor should state whether the GCF proceeds were covered by the scope of the audit.  
 
As a GoG authority, the EPA follows the government’s financial and procurement rules and standards. UNEP has 
undertaken an assessment of the EPA capacity which will guide the procurement monitoring plan which will be agreed 
upon between UNEP and the EPA. T he procurement monitoring plan will be an annex to the PCA and will be shared 
with GCF upon signature, as required by the FAA. The procurement monitoring plan will also be reviewed periodically. 
The project’s investments in equipment will be undertaken fol lowing UN procurement procedures. Finally, in line with 
the UNEP Programme Manual, EPA will be requested to provide an annual compliance audit covering all aspects of 
the project execution, including review of all expenditures incurred during the financial  year.  
 
The procurement plan for the proposed project is provided in Annex 10.  
 
There are no UN sanctions or embargoes listed against EPA or its Management team, and there appears to be no 
evidence of engagement in prohibited practices by EPA. While there is no dedicated act of parliament on corruption, 
Ghana’s penal code penalises co rruption by public officials1 and Ghana has a National Anti -Corruption Action Plan 
(NACAP)1. The Ghana govt also has in place an Anti -Money Laundering Act, 2008 (Act 749)1 which prohibits money 
laundering and established a Financial Intelligence Centre. The Anti -Money Laundering Act penalises money 
laundering and lists it as an extraditable offence.  
Furthermore, the Project Cooperation Agreement with EPA will include a clause requiring EPA to 
contractually oblige all its sub -personnel, agents, employees, or contractors to comply with Anti -Fraud and 
Corruption Framework of the UN and GCF Prohibited Practices.

entity is providing to the Secretariat the follow ing two copies of the funding proposal, including all annexes:  
• full copy for internal use of the GCF in which the confidential portions are marked accordingly, together with 
an explanatory note regarding the said portions and the corresponding reason for confidentiality under the 
accredited entity’s disclosure policy,  and 
• redacted copy for disclosure on the GCF website.  
The funding proposal can only be processed upon receipt of the two copies above, if containing confidential 
information.
