Secretariat’s assessment of FP265 
Proposal name:  Climate -resilient landscapes for sustainable livelihoods in 
northern Ghana  
Accredited entity:  United Nations Environment Programme (UNEP ) 
Country/(ies):  Ghana  
Project/programme size: Medium  
I. Overall assessment of the Secretariat
1. The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following
remarks:
Strengths  Points of caution  
The project targets eight districts in 
northern Ghana  – identified as the most 
climate -vulnerable through data -driven 
analysis and extensive stakeholder 
consultations. By supporting 120 high -risk 
communities with adaptive, locally driven 
interventions, the project ensures targeted 
impact, strengthens resilience , and lays the 
foundation for scalable, climate- resilient 
rural development.  A potential risk is the lack of in -country 
presence of the accredited e ntity, which 
may lead to delays in coordination and 
implementation unless mitigated through 
strong local execution and oversight 
mechanisms.  
All executing entities and partners are 
national institutions, ensuring local 
ownership, institutional alignment, and 
long- term commitment. This nationally led 
model builds and strengthens existing 
systems, supports government coordination, and enhances institutional capacity in Ghana 
to scale and sustain adaptation efforts 
independently.  
2. The Board may wish to consider approving this funding  proposal  in accordance with the
term sheet agreed between the Secretariat and the accredited entity  (AE)  and, if considered
appropriate, subject to the conditions set out in a nnex II to  document GCF/B. 42/02.
II. Summary of the Secretariat’s assessment
2.1 Project background  
3. In northern Ghana, where approximately 80 per cent  of the workforce depends on rain -
fed, subsistence agriculture, smallholder farmers face major challenges due to climat e


---
*Page 87*
---

 
GCF/B. 42/02/Add. 07 
Page 87 
 
 
   
 variability. Prolonged dry seasons cause food shortages and water scarcity, while rising 
temperatures reduce water retention and increase pests and crop spoilage. At the same time, 
extreme rainfall leads to flooding, crop loss, infrastructure damage, and r educed reservoir 
capacity, heightening the risk of dam failure and downstream flooding.  
4. In response, t he project aims to build climate resilience among smallholder farmers in 
northern Ghana by improving food security and strengthening the rural economy. It focuses on 
climate -resilient agriculture and water storage, landscape restoration (e.g. riverbank 
rehabilitation, agroforestry), and climate -resilient rural infrastructure. Activities will be 
implemented at the community level (planning and capacity -building), regional level (weather 
forecasting), and national level (drought and flood action  plan). Implemented over seven years 
in eight climate -vulnerable districts, the project will support 120 communities and provide 
early warning advisories to the wider population. Led by the Ghana Environmental Protection 
Agency (EPA) , in partnership with the Ministry of Food and Agriculture, Ghana Meteorological 
Agency (GMet)  and Water Resources Commission, the project will promote sustainable, climate -
resilient livelihoods through an integrated, ecosystem- based approach . 
5. The proposed funding for the project is USD  70,1 98,822 , utilizing grant  instruments . The 
GCF contribution to the project is USD 63,211,141 in grant . Co-financing will be provided  by the 
Government of Ghana . 
6. The project targets  around 691,125  direct beneficiar ies and around 2.8 million  indirect 
beneficiaries . Together , these beneficiaries make up  9.3 per cent of the whole population of 
Ghana .  
7. GCF categorizes  the pro ject as B (medium ) for its environmental and social safeguards  
(ESS) . 
2.2 Component -by-component analysis  
8. The pro ject is structured in to two components:  
(a) Component 1 : Early warning systems;  and 
(b) Component 2:  Climate -resilient livelihoods.  
Component 1: Early warning systems (total cost: USD  15,358,090 ; GCF cost: USD 13,458 ,090  in 
grants )  
9. This component will strengthen Ghana’s early warning system through a series of 
coordinated interventions. It will improve access to flood and drought information, expand hydrometeorological and groundwater monitoring networks and radar coverage  – particularly 
in the underserved northern region  – to enhance the forecasting accuracy  of GMet , and build 
institutional and technical capacities for drought and flood monitoring, assessment, and 
response. It will also establish a national framework for generating and disseminating climate 
hazard advisories to local communities, supported by the development of a national action plan 
for drought and flood hazard management . 
10. An o utput under this component is improved climate data and early warnings made 
available to facilitate proactive drought and flood management . 
Component 2: Climate -resilient livelihoods (total cost: USD 51,110,644; GCF cost: USD  46,179 ,060  in 
grants )  
11. This component will strengthen capacity for and implement climate -resilient 
agriculture, landscape restoration for drought and flood management, and alternative climate -
resilient livelihoods in beneficiary communities. It will train 200 E xtension Officers (at least 30


---
*Page 88*
---

 
GCF/B. 42/02/Add. 07 
Page 88 
 
 
   
 per cent  women) on climate -resilient practices, ecosystem -based adaptation (EbA), and 
livelihood diversification. Communities will receive training and support to develop or finalize 
their own climate change adaptation plans, based on locally identified priorities and climate risk 
assessments. Adaptation interventions outlined in each climate change adaptation plan – 
selected from a menu of context -specific options – will be implemented with the support of 
District Extension Officers to reduce community vul nerability to climate change.  
12. Outputs under this component are:   
(a) Climate -resilient agricultural practices, EbA and alternative climate -resilient livelihoods 
implemented in beneficiary communitie s;  
(b) Restoration of landscape to reduce drought and flood risk ;  
(c) Increased access of smallholder farmers to financial resources and engagement with the 
private sector ; and 
(d) Knowledge and awareness of climate threats to agricultural livelihoods and available adaptation options increased to inform the upscaling of climate -change adaptation 
across northern Ghana . 
Monitoring and evaluation  (M&E)  (total cost: USD 1 ,392,462 ; GCF cost : USD 1,314,414 ) 
Project management (total cost: USD  2,337,625 ; GCF cost: USD 2,259,577 )  
13. The p roject management budget will finance project management  expenses , such as a 
Project Manager , Finance Officer  and office equipment . 
III. Assessment of performance against investment criteria  
14. The pro ject consistently scores medium  to high against GCF investment criteria. A 
detailed assessment of the project’s fit with GCF investment criteria is provided below.  
3.1 Impact potentia l                           Scale:  Medium  to high 
15. The proposed project has medium to high  impact potential, directly benefiting 691,125  
rural farmers through enhanced early warning systems, adoption of climate- resilient 
agricultural practices, and large -scale agroforestry and restoration activities.  
16. Indirectly, it will reach the broader population of 2.86 million in northern Ghana by 
improving food and water security and increasing resilience to climate hazards. Enhanced 
climate data and early warning dissemination will also strengthen national drought and flood 
response systems, contributing to more climate -responsiv e development planning.  
3.2 Paradigm  shift                              Scale : Medium   
17. The project will catalyse a shift from vulnerable, subsistence farming to climate-
resilient, ecosystem -based livelihoods in northern Ghana. Through the adoption of climate -
smart agriculture, landscape restoration, and diversified income sources, combined w ith 
strengthened early warning systems and planning capacity, communities will move from reactive to proactive climate responses.  
18. Its model is designed for scale -up across 43 districts, supported by a national action 
plan, and integration into local development plans. A strong M&E and knowledge -sharing 
system will document and disseminate lessons, while training and peer -to-peer  learning will 
promote replication and sustained behavioural change beyond the project areas.


---
*Page 89*
---

 
GCF/B. 42/02/Add. 07 
Page 89 
 
 
   
 3.3 Sustainable development potential                         Scale : High  
19. The programme will contribute to several Sustainable Development Goals ( SDGs ): SDG  1 
(No poverty) , SDG 2 ( Zero  hunger ), SDG 8 (Decent work and  economic growth ), SDG 1 3 (Climate 
action ); SDG 15  (Life on land ); and SDG 17 ( Partnerships for the Goals ). The pro gramme  expects 
to realize environmental , social , economic , and gender  co-benefits.  
3.4 Needs of the recipient                                               Scale: High  
20. Northern Ghana is highly vulnerable to climate change due to its reliance on rainfed 
agriculture, limited infrastructure, and a widening development gap. The Government of Ghana 
recognizes the urgency but lacks the fiscal space to fund large- scale adaptati on. Smallholder 
farmers face limited access to credit, and local institutions lack the capacity to implement and 
coordinate adaptation efforts.  
3.5 Country ownership                                                          Scale: High  
21. No-objection letters have been issued by  the Government  of Ghana . Additionally, a ll 
executing entities (EEs) and implementing partners are national entities, with technical support 
and oversight provided by the AE . 
22. The proposed project is fully country -driven as it was prioritiz ed by the n ational 
designated authority in June 2016 using a transparent prioriti zation tool developed under the 
GCF Readiness and Preparatory Support Programme . In addition, extensive consultations with 
national, regional, district and community -level stakeholders (including representatives of local 
government, the private sector and civil society, and women and other vulnerable groups) were 
conducted in -country by the Government of Ghana  during the development of the proposal . 
3.6 Efficiency and effectiveness                                     Scale:  Medium  to high 
23. The project provides grants to strengthen capacities at the national, regional, and field 
levels through training  session s, land restoration , and institutional development  for early 
warning systems . This enables smallholders  to adopt and sustain climate -resilient practices 
beyond the programme implementation period . 
24. The GCF cost of USD 91 per direct beneficiary (and USD 23 per total beneficiary) reflects 
moderate cost -efficiency within the GCF adaptation portfolio. The co -financing ratio of 1:0.11 is 
low; while this  is understandable given the government’s financial constraints and in- kind 
contribution commitments, caution is warranted until these contributions have  fully 
materialized .  
25. Scenario analysis shows that n ature -based infrastructure and h ybrid approaches , such 
as reforestation, climate -smart agriculture, and agroforestry , can substantially reduce climate -
related damages and enhance community resilience. These measures lead to avoided damages 
and generate income benefits, with net benefits of up to USD 55.3 million (W a West) and net 
present values of up to USD 68.8 million under h ybrid scenarios . 
26. The proposed investments are economically sound, with b enefit –cost ratios ranging 
from 1.55 to 2.95 and internal rates of return  from 17.4 to 37.9  per cent  across districts. Even 
under conservative assumptions, the financial returns remain positive. Excluded co -benefits 
such as lives saved, improved nutrition, and carbon sequestration suggest even greater value, underscoring the project ’s strong economic and societal  case .

*Page 90*
---

 
GCF/B. 42/02/Add. 07 
Page 90 
 
 
   
 IV. Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies  
4.1 Environmental and social safeguards  
27. Project overview.  The project aims to enhance the climate resilience of vulnerable 
smallholder farming communities in northern Ghana by improving food security and enhancing 
the ag riculture -based economy. Specifically, the project will improve climate data and early 
warning systems in the Northern region of  Ghana under c omponent 1. Component 2 will focus 
on adoption of climate -resilient agriculture and water storage, and investments in EbA , nature -
based flood -risk reduction, and climate -resilient agricultur al practices, benefiting 15 
communities from each of the 8 districts from 3 regions in n orthern Ghana. The direct 
beneficiaries will be identified based on the selection criteria and stakeholder consultations. 
Beneficiary communities will be able to select the most locally appropriate intervention from a 
menu of adaptation of interventions wh ich has been made available in the project formulation 
phase. The potential environmental and social (E&S) co-benefits include restoration of 
degraded lands leading to increased canopy cover, reduced soil erosion, reduced surface run -off, 
improved ecosystem services and resilience, improved agricultural productivity, and reduced 
exposure to climate -related environmental hazards, and strengthened institutional capacity for 
climate adaptation, among others.  
28. E&S risk category and safeguard instrument.  The proposed project activities have 
been screened against the s afeguards risk identification form of the United Nations 
Environment Programme  (UNEP) and h ave been classified as having moderate risks associated 
with the UNEP safeguards standards on biodiversity, ecosystems and sustainable natural 
resource management and on Indigenous Peoples. The moderate risk classification is equivalent 
to the GCF category B classification and aligned with the accreditation level of the AE. An 
environmental and s ocial assessment management framework (ESAMF) has been developed to 
provide systematic guidelines in further identifying E&S  risks and impacts, mitigating measures, 
and institutional arrangements during project implementation. Th e following potential risks 
have been identified in the ESAMF: i) introduction of harmful species; ii) increased use of 
agricultural chemicals; iii) increased demand for irrigation; iv) habitat conversion; v) excessive 
fire suppression; and v) the adverse effects of on -farm earthworks. The ESAMF identifies 
mitigating measures following the hierarchy of mitigation.   
29. Compliance with GCF ESS standards . The following paragraphs describe how the 
project complies with the se standards.  
30. ESS 1: Assessment and management of E&S risks and impacts . Since the exact 
locations and design of specific activities will be identified during implementation, the AE has prepared an ESAMF  at funding proposal development stage. The project’s ES AMF provides 
guidelines for the screening and assessment of potential E&S  risks and impacts; identifies 
potential E&S  risks and impacts and corresponding indicative mitigating measures; describes 
the implementation arrangements and capacity needs; provides details fo r the establishment of 
a grievance redress mechanism  (GRM) ; identifies information disclosure requirements; and 
describes the stakeholder engagement process during the project development and 
implementation phases. The ES AMF also sets out a ctivity exclusion criteria and p rohibited 
activities, outright excluding activities that may have significant E&S  risks and impacts 
equivalent to a category A project. During implementation, any subproject categorized as B 
would require a fit -for-purpose environmental and  social impact assessment  and environmental 
and social management plan  which would need to be disclosed following the GCF Information 
Disclosure Policy. Compliance with national regulatory requirements will also be met, as 
required. Monitoring indicators have been developed for the ES AMF implementation and 
results will be reflected in the annual performance reports.


---
*Page 91*
---

 
GCF/B. 42/02/Add. 07 
Page 91 
 
 
   
 31. ESS 2: Labour and working conditions . The engagement of project staff and 
contractors will be undertaken in compliance with national labour laws and international 
commitments, such as I nternational Labour Organization  conventions, as well as UNEP and GCF 
procurement polic ies and principles. This will ensure that staff are not subjected to adverse 
working conditions, occupational health and safety risks, forced labour and child labour. The 
Ghana Labour Act of 2003 provides general labour protections, however , gaps with 
international good practice have been identified such as those regarding grievance redress mechanisms for workers and compliance with contractor obligations. As such, the UNEP 
safeguard standards will be applied to fill the gaps. A mandatory c ode of c onduct will be signed 
by contractors and project staff. The project anticipates engagement of local labour only and no 
work camp establishment is envisaged. While no significant occupational health and safety risks 
are anticipated, measures will be in place to address any potential risks through EPA 
regulations and good practice. For interventions involving physical work, appropriate health 
and safety training, equipment and safety measures will be in place.  
32. ESS 3: Resource efficiency and pollution prevention . Early w arning infrastructure 
could cause construction -related impacts such as increased noise and dust levels, localized soil 
and vegetation disturbance, and contamination of nearby water sources. For all activities , the 
AE has identified this standard as triggered with a low level of risk. On o utput 1, as the 
installation of small -scale early  warning equipment involves  construction works, detailed 
mitigation measures relating to construction works will h ave to be detailed in the site- specific 
environmental and social management plan s once the target sites have been identified. On 
output 2, the promotion of climate -resilient agricultural techniques could increase water 
demand and encourage the use of fertilizers and pesticides. Waste can also be generated from 
bags  and containers from agricultural activities. To mitigate such risks, the project will provide 
farmers with the capacity to undertake  integrated pest management, including training on the 
safe use of agrochemicals and waste management, storage, application, minimization and 
disposal. Mitigation measures should be adopted to address potential risks and impacts related 
to climate -resilient water infrastructure (boreholes and check dams). The a ctivity exclusion 
criteria and p rohibit ed activities list  in the E environmental  and Social Assessment and 
Management Framework  identif y activities likely to generate significant greenhouse gas 
emissions, or pollution beyond permissible limits, including uncontrolled burning or industrial 
emissions. No substantial consumption of energy, water or other material inputs associated 
with the pro ject are anticipated. To mitigate potential impacts from small- scale climate- resilient 
livelihood promotion, the project will disseminate good practice guidance on pollution 
prevention, resource efficiency, and environmentally sound waste disposal tailored  to livelihood 
activities. Low -input, low -pollution processing techniques through technical support and 
extension services will be promoted. Chemicals and materials subject to international bans and 
phase -outs will not be used during the proposed project interventions.  
33. ESS 4: Community health, safety and security . Minimal health, safety and security 
risks are associated with the installation of early warning observation equipment and the 
potential for intergroup conflict between sedentary and pastoralist groups if benefits from project interventions are not managed well. The risks associated with local conflict will be 
reassessed and addressed at the local level through customary  land -sharing practices and 
participatory planning processes during implementation to ensure t hat appropriate mitigating 
measures are adopted.  Some sites for observation equipment may need to be fenced  to limit 
public access for safety purposes. The project activities are not expected to result in an influx of workers requiring worker camps, use of security services, nor will they involve medium to 
large -scale construction. Health and safety risks related to livestock -rearing are minimal, as the 
project does not involve the provision or scaling -up of animal -husbandry activities, and no 
increase in a nimal populations is anticipated. Likewise, the project does not include large -scale 
water infrastructure or open water sources . Borehole installations  will be small  in scale and  will


---
*Page 92*
---

 
GCF/B. 42/02/Add. 07 
Page 92 
 
 
   
 be screened to ensure they do not generate additional risks related to water  or vector -borne 
diseases.   
34. ESS 5: Land acquisition and involuntary resettlement . The risks are assessed to be of 
low significance on land acquisition and involuntary resettlement. No physical or economic 
displacement is anticipated from the project activities. Early warning observation equipment 
will be installed on government land as much as possible. In case it is found not  to be  feasible to 
install on government -owned land, formal lease on private land will be arranged through a 
participatory process. If the land identified is within pastoralist grazing land or traditionally  
owned and used land, land access will be agreed through a fully consultative process (i.e. free, 
prior and informed consent  in the case of Indigeno us Peoples’ involvement). To ensure that 
pastoralists are not negatively affected, the project will not support any activity that results in changes in land -tenure arrangements or change existing land -use restrictions. The project will 
not support any activity that would lead to forced eviction.  
35. ESS 6: Biodiversity conservation and sustainable management of living natural 
resources.  While E&S  risks from a single intervention may be considered as low risk, 
collectively the project is assessed as  having moderate risks triggering the UNEP Safeguards 
Standard on Biodiversity, Ecosystems and Sustainable Natural Resource Management. The implementation of climate change adaptation interventions, including climate- resilient 
agricultural practices may affect local ecosystems if risks are not  managed properly. In selecting 
sites for any activity, siting within or near protected habitats, areas supporting endangered 
species, and areas of high conservation value will be stringently  avoided. The project will only 
support agricultural production in existing agricultural lands used by communities. In planning for plantation reforestation, the project will ensure alignment with any existing management 
plans and land -use plans, and will exclude support for any monoculture practice. No invasive 
species or genetically modified organism will be supported. As part of capacity -building 
initiatives, biodiversity conservation and risk management training practices will be incorporated in to the training materials to enhance the long -term sustainability of climate -
resilient agriculture.  
36. GCF Indigeno us Peoples Policy and ESS 7 on Indigenous Peoples : The AE foresees 
potential impact on Indigenous Peoples in regard to water, land and natural resources access. 
The AE has started initial  engagement with representatives of transhumant pastoralists and 
provided an Indigenous Peoples Planning Framework, which requests systematic screening for Indigenous Peoples , consultations and ensuring free, prior and informed consent  when needed. 
The project has excluded any change to customary land use, or restriction of use to traditional lands used by pastoralists. The AE foresees Indigenous Peoples representation at the Project 
Steering Committee. The AE is recommended to engage wi th the GCF Indigenous  Peoples 
Advisory Group on relevant I ndigenous Peoples' issues that may be encountered.  
37. ESS 8: Cultural heritage . The proposed activities will not be located in any known 
cultural heritage sites and as such, no adverse impacts on cultural heritage are anticipated. 
However, since the project involves earthworks, chance find procedures will be adopted as a 
precautiona ry measure.  
38. Implementation arrangements . Under the oversight role of UNEP as accredited entity, 
the EPA of the Ministry of Environment, Science, Technology and Innovation as the main EE will 
be responsible for the overall implementation of the ES AMF. EPA will be guided by a Project 
Steering Committee composed of representatives of  various government agencies, United 
Nations Capital Development Fund , and civil society organizations . GMet will serve as the 
executing entity for output 1 on improving climate data and early warning systems. E&S focal 
points will be designated from eac h executing entity to manage safeguard implementation at 
operational level. To ensure effective safeguards and GRM implementation, the E&S focal points will be provided with training and technical support from EPA and the Gender and Safeguards


---
*Page 93*
---

 
GCF/B. 42/02/Add. 07 
Page 93 
 
 
   
 Specialist at the Project Management Unit ( PMU ) level. E&S  training will also be provided for all 
staff involved in project implementation at the national, regional and district levels .  
39. Stakeholder engagement . Multilevel stakeholder engagements involving 
representatives of the public and private sectors, women and farmer -focused non -governmental 
organizations, and the target ed local communities, including farmers, have been conducted by 
the AE together with the EE during project design and formulation. The stakeholder 
engagements aimed at identifying the needs, issues, risks and resolutions associated with the 
project activitie s. Key concerns raised pertain to GRMs , gender dynamics and inclusion of ethnic 
minorities. The key concerns raised will be addressed through the implementation of the 
ESAMF, Indigenous Peoples Planning Framework , Gender Assessment and Action Plan  and the 
subsequent plans to be developed during implementation. The PMU will continue with the stakeholder consultations during project implementation as detailed in the s takeholder 
engagement plan.  
40. Grievance redress mechanism . A project -supported GRM  that builds on existing 
structures will be made available to all project stakeholders, including contractors or contracted labourers in addition to an GRM process specific to Indigenous Peoples, which will be designed, 
in a consultative manner with potentially affected I ndigenous Peoples  groups. In addition, the 
institutional GRM  of the AE  (Stakeholder Response Mechanism) and the GCF Independent 
Redress Mechanism will also be made available to stakeholder s as additional formal channels. 
The GRM will not impede access to judicial or administrative remedies that may be relevant or 
applicable. The GRM will be disseminated to all stakeholders through meetings, printed posters 
and flyers in local languages post ed in community centres , assembly halls, and extension offices. 
Full training will be provided to project implementers on the GRM implementation.  
41. Sexual exploitation, abuse and harassment  safeguarding : In accordance with the 
Revised Environmental and Social Policy ( decision B.BM -2021/18 ), the AE has acknowledged 
sexual exploitation, abuse, and h arassment (SEAH) as a relevant safeguard issue and has 
included risk mitigation measures in both its gender action plan and ESAMF. SEAH is recognized 
as a contextual risk due to project  team –community interaction, gender and power 
asymmetries, and the participation of economically and socially vulnerable groups. Mitigation 
measures include mandatory SEAH prevention training for project staff, community climate 
action groups, farmer -based organizations, and targeted community members.  The AE has also 
committed to conducting a dedicated SEAH risk assessment at district level during the project’s 
inception phase. This assessment will inform context -specific mitigation planning and allow for 
early validation of reporting mechanisms , stakeholder sensitivities, and risk exposure across the 
target areas. The approach to SEAH risk mitigation is consistent with the provisions of the GCF 
SEAH Policy. Institutional responsibilities are clearly defined, and awareness -raising measures 
are em bedded across relevant stakeholder levels. Training measures include both in -person and 
online delivery. The integration of SEAH considerations within gender, safeguard, and financial 
access components is noted.  The planned development of the SEAH -specific  GRM  during the 
inception phase presents an opportunity to ensure that such systems are accessible, confidential 
and culturally appropriate.  
4.2 Gender policy  
42. The AE provided a gender assessment and action plan with the funding proposal and 
therefore, complies with the requirements of the updated GCF Gender Policy.  A gender 
assessment has been conducted, drawing on a desk review and stakeholder consultations, and 
contextualized within the national gender policy environment. It identifies key gender -related 
barriers such as unequal access to land, limited financial inclusion, disparities in educational 
attainment, and underrepresentation in decision -making structu res. These barriers reduce the


---
*Page 94*
---

 
GCF/B. 42/02/Add. 07 
Page 94 
 
 
   
 adaptive capacity of women and other vulnerable groups by limiting their ability to participate 
in, benefit from, and influence climate -resilient livelihood strategies. The assessment further 
notes the influence of entrenched gender norms and customary lan d tenure systems, which 
shape control over resources and reinforce gendered divisions of labo ur across sectors such as 
agriculture, shea processing and petty trade. It also considers intersectional factors, highlighting 
how overlapping vulnerabilities , such as disability, age and informal employment , compound 
exclusion from economic opportunities and climate adaptation efforts.  
43. A gender action plan is provided. It presents a structured approach to addressing 
gender -related risks and opportunities. It outlines gender -responsive activities under each of 
the project’s outputs and includes associated indicators, sex -disaggregated targets, timelines, 
roles and responsibilities, and estimated budgets. A Safeguards and Gender Specialist is 
expected to be embedded within the PMU to oversee the implementation, monitoring and 
coordination of gender -related actions , supported by institutiona l capacity from EPA, Ministry 
of Food and Agriculture , and other project partners . Additional capacity -building measures and 
training activities on gender mainstreaming are planned at the community and institutional 
levels.  
44. Gender -responsive design elements are incorporated into various project components. 
These include support for women -led cooperatives, targeted financial literacy and mentorship 
program mes, participation in early warning systems, and leadership roles in knowledge- sharing 
initiatives. The proposal also outlines efforts to promote inclusive engagement in district -level 
planning processes and to ensure participation in decision -making.  The use of community -level 
baselines, targeted leadership roles, and monitor ing mechanisms suggests a commitment to 
tracking both participation and transformative outcomes. The Secretariat encourages the AE to 
continue prioritizing disaggregated impact tracking and documentation of lessons learned 
during implementation . 
4.3 Risks  
4.3.1.  AE/EE capability  to execute the current project ( medium risk)  
45. The UNEP will serve as the AE for this project. Currently, there are 10 approved FPs 
with UNEP, with a total committed amount of USD249 million in the GCF portfolio. The AE will 
collaborate with two E Es, which are government agencies namely, EPA and Gmet. Since UNEP 
does not have an in -country presence in Ghana  and will be managing from their regional office , 
the capacity of the EEs will be crucial, as they will be responsible for directly managing the day -
to-day operations of the project.   
4.3.2.  Programme specific execution risk  (medium risk ) 
46. Monitoring of the impact for engaging financial institutions (FIs): Output 4 aims to 
improve smallholder farmers’ access to finance by building capacity, forming farmer -based 
organizations (FBOs) and Village Savings and Lans Associations (VSLAs), and linking them to 
blended finance initiatives by international and local FIs. While there are detailed plans and 
analyses of existing facilities and stakeholders, the project scope is limited to the establishment 
of FBOs/VSLAs, and a blended finance model, rather  than resulting credit lines or loan 
agreements - these are framed as potential exit strategies without clear indicators, making 
monitoring difficult. This limitation in scope highlights the need for robust monitoring 
mechanisms to ensure that the intended  outcomes are achieved and to assess the effectiveness 
of capacity -building efforts  as stipulated in the M&E plan.  
47. Use of grant: As mentioned above, Output 4 may involve on -lending activities. Given that 
GCF provides a full grant, there should be a clear distinction in terms of use of grant and loans.


---
*Page 95*
---

 
GCF/B. 42/ 02/Add. 07 
Page 95 
 
 
   
 To mitigate this, the AE has confirmed that GCF proceeds will not be used to finance credit lines 
or guarantees through financial intermediaries. This assurance is crucial in maintaining the 
integrity of the project's financial management and ensuring that  funds are utilized as intended 
to support smallholder farmers directly.  
4.3.3.  GCF’s portfolio concentration risk  
48. In case of approval, the impact of this proposal on the GCF portfolio risk remains non -
material and within the risk appetite in terms of concentration levels, results area, single 
proposal or AE concentration.  
4.3.4.  Compliance risk (medium risk)  
49. Certain project activities, such as the establishment of village savings and loan 
associations and a blended finance model to provide credit lines – though GCF funds will not be 
used to finance credit lines and guarantees through financial intermediaries – present elevated 
ML/TF risks.     
50. The AE has assessed ML/TF risk as low given its internal controls, including its risk -
based approach to engaging implementing partners on the basis of assessments of their capacity 
and governance capabilities, as well as its established monitoring and eval uation procedures 
and financial reporting mechanism, which will be applied to this project. Moreover, the AE will provide anti -money laundering/countering the financing of terrorism (AML/CFT) training to 
downstream partners at the start of the project. In addition, the AE’s capacity assessment of EPA found that it has the requisite fiduciary and financial management capacity, including in relation 
to AML/CFT compliance.    
51. Based on the AE’s internal controls and governance mechanisms that will be applied 
during project implementation, and the relatively elevated ML/TF risks associated with certain 
activities, the overall compliance risk is determined to be medium.

4.4 Fiduciary  
52. As the AE, UNEP will provide fiduciary oversight and safeguard supervision, ensuring 
compliance with both UNEP and GCF policies. UNEP will oversee the full project cycle – 
including formulation, start -up, implementation, and closure – using its internal programme 
manual, which includes robust provisions for financial management, procurement, and project oversight. A UNEP Task Manager will lead project supervision and undertake at least two 
oversight missions annually. The experience of UNEP in Ghana, including as d elivery partner for 
the National Adaptation Plan Readiness project and in implementing EbA initiatives and 
environmental programmes, underscores its strong technical and fiduciary capabilities and 
institutional presence in the country.  
53. Executing functions will be carried out by Ghana’s EPA and GMet, with UNEP signing 
formal p roject cooperation agreements (PCAs) with each entity. These PCAs will establish clear 
roles, fiduciary obligations, disbursement conditions, and reporting requirements, aligned with 
UNEP operational standards. The EPA, with a proven track record as an executing agency under


---
*Page 96*
---

 
GCF/B. 42/02/Add. 07 
Page 96 
 
 
   
 Global Environment Facility -funded programmes (e.g. GEMP, SLWMP), has undergone UNEP 
fiduciary and capacity assessments, confirming its adequacy in financial and operational 
management. GMet, responsible for climate and hydrological data, will implement o utput 2, and 
both entities will be held accountable for results and the effective use of project resources.  
54. The project governance structure includes a national- level Project Steering Committee, 
co-chaired by UNEP and the Ministry of Environment, Science, Technology and Innovation, and 
a PMU housed within the regional office of the EPA  in Tamale. The PMU will be staffed with a 
dedicated Financial Officer, Procurement/Administrative Officer, and an M&E specialist, and 
will work closely with the  finance and procurement teams  of UNEP . Regional implementation 
will be supported by l ocal steering committees, and coordination with national ministries and 
district authorities will ensure vertical alignment and oversight. The EPA will serve as the Project Steering Committee  secretariat and lead the day -to-day fiduciary oversight of EEs  and 
implementing partners.  
55. At the community level, implementation will be supported by a gricultural extension 
agents and local institutions such as c limate change action groups and farmer -based 
organizations, ensuring resource flows and information are responsive to local needs. UNEP -
DHI will also be engaged through a PCA to deliver technical support using internationally recognized modelling  tools. Memorandums of understanding  among entities such as EPA –
Ministry if Food and Agriculture  and GMet –Water Resources Commission  will define inter -
agency roles in agricultural and hydrological interventions. This layered and contractually 
governed approach ensures a clear chain of accountability, appropriate use of GCF funds, and 
compliance with fiduciary standards across all level s of implementation.  
4.5 Results monitoring and reporting  
56. The proposed project aims to contribute towards three adaptation result s areas , namely, 
the increased resilience of most vulnerable people and communities; health, well- being, food 
securi ty, and water security; and ecosystems and ecosystem services. To report on the 
corresponding achievements, the proposal included in its logical framework five adaptation 
indicators:   
(a) Core indicator 2: Direct and indirect beneficiaries reached ;  
(b) Supplementary 2.1: Beneficiaries (female/male) adopting improved and/or new 
climate -resilient livelihood options ;  
(c) Supplementary 2.2: Beneficiaries (female/male) with improved food security ;  
(d) Supplementary 2.4: Beneficiaries (female/male) covered by new or improved early 
warning systems ; and  
(e) Core indicator 4: Hectares of natural resource areas brought under improved low -
emission and/or climate -resilient management practices.  
57. These results will be achieved by reducing the vulnerability to climate change of 
smallholder farmers in northern Ghana through the implementation and adoption of climate -
resilient agriculture, EbA  approaches, alternative climate- resilient livelihoods and locally led 
investments in climate resilient rural infrastructure.  The project theory of change articulates the 
results pathways that will lead to the improved adaptive capacity of landscape- level ecosystems 
to flood, drought, and high temperature in the project site.  
58. The project’s M&E plan presents details of data sources and data collection and 
analytical methods  for each indicator required by the Integrated Results Management 
Framework , including periodic surveys undertaken at the start, mid dle, and end  of the project 
implementation , Geographical Information System  mapping, independent verification of


---
*Page 97*
---

 
GCF/B. 42/02/Add. 07 
Page 97 
 
 
   
 reported results, focus group discussions, key informant interviews, and analysis of secondary 
information based on government data. The plan also allocated budget for a dedicated 
Monitoring and R eporting Officer. The overall M&E budget is around USD 2,337,625 , which the 
Secretariat deems sufficient to meet the requirements  of the monitoring and accountability 
framework , Integrated Results Management Framework , and GCF Evaluation Policy. The main 
risk to reporting credible results is the potential impact of any locali zed conflict that could affect 
the feasibility and/or cost of data collection on the ground.  
4.6 Legal assessment  
59. The Accreditation Master Agreement, which was signed on 15 December 2016 and 
became effective on 20 February 2017, was subsequently amended and restated by First Amendment and Restatement Agreement which was signed on 13 July 2023 and became 
effective on 2  August 2023 (as so amended and restated, the "AMA"),  
60. The Accredited Entity has provided certificate confirming that it has obtained all 
internal approvals and it has the capacity and authority to implement the project.   
61. The proposed project will be implemented in the Republic of Ghana, country in which 
GCF is not provided with privileges and immunities. This means that, amongst other things, GCF 
is not protected against litigation or expropriation in this country, which r isks need to be 
further assessed. Moreover, the ability of GCF to undertake redress activities and/or 
investigations in this country may be hindered due to the absence of privileges and immunities 
for relevant GCF personnel.  
62. Therefore, it is recommended that the Board considers whether disbursements of GCF 
proceeds should only be made after GCF has obtained satisfactory protection against litigation 
and expropriation in the country, or has been provided with appropriate privileges and 
immunities for GCF and its personnel . 
63. To address the matters raised in this section, it is recommended that any approval by 
the Board is made subject to the following conditions:  
(a) Signature of the funded activity agreement in a form and substance satisfactory to the GCF Secretariat within 180 days from the date of Board approval; and  
(b) Completion of the legal due diligence to the satisfaction of the GCF Secretariat.

Independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment of FP265 
Proposal name:  Climate -resilient landscapes for sustainable livelihoods in 
northern Ghana  
Accredited entity:  United Nations Environment Programme ( UNEP ) 
Country/(ies):  Ghana  
Project/programme size: Medium  
I. Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel
1.1 Overview  
1. In Northern Ghana agriculture employs roughly 80% of the region’s workforce and is
predominantly rainfed and relies on traditional farming methods and assessed to have “limited
technological inputs.” This dependence on natural climate conditions leaves far mers highly
exposed to environmental shifts. The region’s agroecological systems are under strain due to acombination of factors, including erratic climate linked weather patterns, environment
al
d
egradation from unsustainable land use, rapid population gr owth, and disruptions caused by
dam spills from the Tamne and Bagre reservoirs.
2. Exacerbating impact of climate risk on the development  pathway.  Climate change is
intensifying these pressures. Key trends include shorter and more unpredictable rainy seasons ,
i
ncreased frequency of extreme rainfall events, prolonged dry spells, rising temperatures, and
heightened evapotranspiration rates. These incr easing climate hazards have cascading effects
such as degraded soils, reduced crop yields, loss of arable land, and more frequent bushfires.
Farmers already operating at subsistence levels stru ggle to adapt, leading to worsening food
insecurity, income instability, and deepening poverty. Without targeted interventions such a s
imp
roved climate information, resilient farming techniques, and sustainable resource
management the farmers’ capacity to cope will further weaken .
3. Need for strategic interventions.  The situation demands urgent action because if left
unaddressed, climate -induced agricultural decline will worsen marginalization of rural
communities, exacerbate food shortages, and destabilize economic activities in northern Ghan a.
Th
us, effective solut ions need integration of climate adaptation, environmental restoration, and
livelihood diversification to build long -term resilience for smallholder farmers.
4. Proposed project. The Climate -Resilient Landscapes for Sustainable Livelihoods
project in Northern Ghana, aims to enhance climate adaptation, improve agricultural resilience ,
an
d integrate climate -smart practices across vulnerable smallholder farming communities.
5. P roject objective and outcomes. As stated in the funding proposal, the “proposed
project objective is to enhance the climate resilience of vulnerable smallholder farming
communities in northern Ghana by improving food security and enhancing the agro -based rural
economy. The project’s outcomes will strengthen institutional and regulatory systems forclimate -responsive planning and development, enabling government agencies and loc
al
i
nstitutions to effectively integrate climate adaptation into policies and s trategies. The project
supports the adoption of climate -resilient agricultural practices, alternative livelihoods, and
access to financial resources, ensuring farmers can sustainably adapt to climate impacts.Additionally, the project improves knowledge an d learning processes, fostering informed


---
*Page 99*
---

 
GCF/B. 42/02/Add. 07 
Page 99 
 
 
decision -making and empowering communities with the tools and information needed to scale 
up climate adaptation efforts across the region.”1 
6. Project components and outputs. The project is divided into two components, namely 
early -warning systems (Component 1) and climate resilient livelihoods (Component 2). The 
outputs (strategic interventions) are as follows: (i) improved climate data and early warnings 
made available for fa cilitating proactive management, (ii) climate -resilient agricultural 
practices, EbA and alternative livelihoods implemented in beneficiary communities, (iii) 
restoration of landscape to reduce droughts and flood risks, (iv) increased access of smallholder 
farmers to financial resources, and (v) increased knowledge and awareness of climate threats to 
agricultural livelihoods and available adaptation options.2  
7. Project summary. This climate adaptation initiative represents a strategic investment 
in sustainable agricultural development, with a total project budget of USD 70,198,882 
(including GCF grant funding of USD 63,211,141 and co -financing). This is a medium- scale 
project tha t has a 7 -year implementation period designed to establish foundational climate 
resilience systems, and a 28 -year lifespan to ensure lasting impacts for vulnerable farming 
communities. Grant financing (USD 63,211,141) is the funding modali ty to ensure accessibility 
for resource -constrained smallholders.3 
1.2 Impact potential                                     Scale:   High  
8. The project is meant  to improve resilience through investments in nature -based 
solutions such as riparian buffer restoration, agroforestry, and adaptive agricultural techniques. 
It also seeks to facilitate financial access for smallholder farmers through climate- resilient 
financing models, ensuring long -term viability beyond the funding period. Early warning 
systems are planned to be developed to enhance protection of communities against climate -
induced disasters, further strengthening adaptive capacity.  
9. Ecosystem resilience through nature-based solutions. The project is expected to 
enhance ecosystem resilience in northern Ghana by deploying large -scale Ecosystem -based 
Adaptation (EbA) strategies. The planned restoration of 14,000 hectares of degraded land and 
2,000 hectares of riparian zones across eight di stricts is projected to reduce flood risks by 
nearly 47%, protect watersheds, and enhance soil and water retention. Other reforestation 
efforts using climate -resilient indigenous tree species and natural regeneration aims to   reverse 
degradation. Agroforestry interventions in 120 communities are anticipated to benefit over 
120,000 people. Fire management practices are designed to reduce the frequency and severity 
of bushfires exacerbated by increasing te mperatures or dry seasons, further securing critical 
agroecosystems. These combinations of actions are intended to restore ecological integrity, 
support biodiversity, and buffer communities from increasing variability, frequency and 
intensity of climate ex treme events.  
10. Resilient livelihoods and climate -smart agriculture (CSA).  The project introduces 
CSA practices to address climate -induced agricultural risks (e.g., erratic rainfall, extended 
droughts, and crop failure).  The adoption of drought -resistant crops, precision irrigation, and 
agroforestry models is expected to promote  higher yield stability and sustainable resource use. 
These measures are designed to safeguard food security and stabilize the incomes of 
smallholder farmers. The restoration of natural floodplains  and creation of buffer zones aim to 
protect farmland and infrastructure, ensuring sustained agricultural productivity despite intensifying climate change and variability.  
 
1 See funding proposal section B.2  
2 See funding proposal section B.3  
3 See funding proposal sections A.7 to A.11.


---
*Page 100*
---

 
GCF/B. 42/02/Add. 07 
Page 100 
 
 
11. Financial inclusion as resilience measure.  The project seeks to enhance farmers’ 
creditworthiness, collective bargaining power, and other structural barriers to financial 
inclusion by organizing smallholder farmers into Farmer -Based Organizations (FBOs) and 
Village Savings and Loans Associations (V SLAs). Through capacity building and financial literacy 
training, farmers are expected to gain skills in accessing credit, insurance, and investments in 
climate -resilient practices. Strategic partnerships with local financial institutions, supported by 
a blended finance model, are projected to expand access to tailored financial products while de-
risking lending activities. Digital financial services are planned to reach remote communities, 
with special provisions intended to empower women through targeted tools and training. This 
financial empowerment is anticipated to underpin long -term economic resilience and inclusive 
rural development.  
12. Upgrade of infrastructure protection and early warning systems.  The project aims 
to strengthen the resilience of rural infrastructure and communities through the development of localized, climate -informed early warning systems (CIEWS). CIEWS are designed to improve 
disaster preparedness and risk management in areas vulnerable to floods and droughts. By integrating meteorological and hydrological data with community -based dissemination 
channels, the project seeks to enhance protection of lives, assets, and p roductivity from climate 
shocks. Infrastructure such as roads, water facilities, and schools frequently damaged by climate extreme events is expected to benefit from improved adaptation planning and risk reduction 
measures.  
13. Emissions reduction co -benefits.  While primarily adaptation -focused, the project is 
expected to generate substantial mitigation co -benefits through sustainable land management 
and agro -ecological farming practices. Reforestation and agroforestry activities are projected to 
sequester appro ximately 1.2 million tCO₂e over the project’s lifetime. These interventions align 
with Ghana’s broader climate goals and offer long -term enviro nmental dividends without 
compromising the project’s core adaptation objectives.  
14. Broad and inclusive impact.  Overall, the project is expected to directly benefit 
approximately 691,125 individuals, with an additional 2.8 million people indirectly impacted 
through improved resilience measures. By focusing on inclusive, nature -based, and financially 
viable solutions , the project aims to build the adaptive capacity of communities, ecosystems, and 
institutions in a region facing acute climate risks. The project is designed to lay the groundwork 
for transformational resilience across northern  Ghana.  
15. The funding proposal’s impact potential is assessed as high.  
1.3 Paradigm shift potential                   Scale:  Medium to high  
16. Enabling Ghana Meteorological Agency ( GMET ) transformation into a modern 
meteorological service through digital innovation.   A successful integration or adoption of 
latest technologies (e.g., satellite remote sensing and AI -powered data fusion algorithms) in 
Output 1 (i.e., activities 1.1.1 and 1.1.2)4 presents a transformative opportunity to modernize 
the GMET  and enhance its service delivery.  GMET  can potentially improve forecast accuracy 
through the intelligent merging of ground -based Hydromet data with satellite observations. 
This scientific advancement is designed to feed into a user -friendly web portal, making seasonal 
forecasts and climate risk information accessible to farmers, policymakers, and disaster 
managers in actionable formats.  
17. The web -based Decision Support System (DSS) sets out to revolutionize early warning 
capabilities by integrating flood and drought monitoring, water allocation models, and risk 
assessments into a single platform. Combined with automated SMS/email alerts and  cloud 
 
4 See funding proposal section B.2.


---
*Page 101*
---

 
GCF/B. 42/02/Add. 07 
Page 101 
 
 
computing for real -time data processing, GMET  is poised to deliver timely, location -specific 
warnings directly to at -risk communities. Key digital components which may include 
centralized web portals, AI -driven analytics, and geospatial visualization tools are designed to 
work together to convert com plex climate data into practical, decision -ready information.  
18. This potential digital transformation aligns GMET  with global best practices in CSA and 
disaster risk reduction, while addressing critical gaps in last -mile information delivery. By 
transitioning from traditional methods to an integrated, technology -driven approach, GMET  can 
position itself as a modern meteorological service capable of meeting 21st century climate 
challenges turning raw data into life- saving and livelihood -protecting actions for Ghana's most 
vulnerable populations. The result will hopefully  be a more responsive, precise, and user -
centered national meteorological agency that leverages cutting -edge technology to fulfil  its 
mandate more effectively than ever before.  
19. Activities 1.4 and 1.5 plan for information dissemination and a national plan for 
coordinating drought and flood hazard management. There is however no mention about how to develop preparedness and response capabilities. It would be advisable to ensure that all 
elements of an Early Warning System be in place and well- aligned with the needs of farmers and 
other groups to which the system is to cater for.  
20. CIEWS scaling up.  The project outlines a  strong potential for scaling up climate -
informed early warning systems in northern Ghana by addressing key gaps in coverage, data 
accuracy, and information dissemination. Existing drought and flood warnings are limited in 
reach and effectiveness particularly in vulnerable rural areas. The project proposes expanding 
hydrometeorological monitoring, enhancing radar coverage, and training national and local 
agencies  in advanced forecasting and response especially GMet . A national framework will  
hopefully guide the generation and community -level dissemination of climate risk information, 
and its planned integration into district planning and budgeting systems will support long -term 
sustainability. The plan to embe d climate adaptation into local development plans and 
establishing financing mechanisms enhances the project ’s potential for a replicable CIEWS 
model worthy of a  nationwide scale- up. 
21. Accelerating a paradigm shift towards climate -resilient agricultural economy in 
Northern Ghana. The proposed project presents a transformative opportunity to shift northern 
Ghana's agriculture from a vulnerable, rainfed, and unsustainable system toward a low -carbon, 
climate -resilient economy. The integrated approach combining climate -resilient agricu lture 
(CRA), ecosystem -based adaptation (EbA), and locally led investments in rural infrastructure 
addresses both environmental and socio -economic vulnerabiliti es. CRA practices such as 
drought -resistant crops, sustainable livestock systems, and water -efficient irrigation will 
stabilize yields under shifting climatic conditions. Parallel EbA interventions (i.e., reforestation, 
riverbank restoration, agroforestry,  and fire management) will restore ecosystem services, 
reduce land degradation, and buffer communities against floods and droughts.  
22. The introduction of alternative, climate -resilient livelihoods and strengthened local 
climate advisory services enables farmers to move away from environmentally harmful practices like slash -and-burn farming. Investments in meteorological infrastructure an d early 
warning systems will improve climate risk forecasting and inform adaptive decisions at the 
farm level. Institutional strengthening through district -level technical assistance, integration of 
adaptation into development plans, and climate finance ac cess will also enable greening of the 
public financial management and the sustainability and scalability of these interventions. These 
strategies create the conditions for a paradigm shift; empowering smallholder communities to 
adopt resilient practices, i ncrease productivity, and build a more sustainable rural economy 
aligned with national climate goals.  
1.3.1.  Risk to paradigm shift potential

26. T ransformative Gender Empowerment.   The project’s Gender Assessment and Action
Plan (GAAP) empowers women not merely as participants, but as active agents of change in
climate -resilient development. It promotes women’s leadership in FBOs, Village Savings and
Loan Associations (VSLAs), and Community Climate Action Groups, positioning them in role s
t
raditionally dominated by men. Women are equipped with practical skills in climate -resilient
agriculture, land restoration, and ecosystem -based adaptation, and are supported to acce ss
f
inancial services including credit and insurance through tailored financial literacy and
enterprise development training. The GAAP also facilitates women's engagement in valu e
ch
ains, linking them to markets and building their capacity to manage climate -resilient
livelihoods. By addressing structural barriers such as limited access to land, finance, anddecision -making spaces, the GAAP ensures that women are not only included but also
empowered to drive long -term climate adaptation a nd economic resilience in their
communities.
5
27. Socio-Economic Development.  The project has strong potential to drive inclusive
socio -economic development, particularly among Indigenous Peoples, women, youth, and rur al
co
mmunities in northern Ghana. Guided by the GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy, an Indigenou s
P
eoples Planning Frame work (IPPF) has been developed to ensure meaningful engagement
throughout the project lifecycle. This includes mapping indigenous communities, respecting
customary decision -making processes, and preparing site- specific Indigenous  Peoples Plans
(IPPs) to align project interventions with community priorities. These processes ensure thatindigenous people’s rights, perspectives, and development opportunities are fully integrated
and safeguarded.
28. The project will strengthen livelihoods for approximately 142,000 farming households
by increasing agricultural productivity, improving post -harvest processing, and promoting
value -added activities. This will enhance rural incomes, reduce food losses, and diversify market
opportunities. Women, who make up at least 50% of beneficiaries, will be empowered through
targeted support for alternative livelihoods such as shea butter production, beekeeping, andsmall livestock rearing; helping to shift gender roles and improve household resilience. Youth
and women will also be involved in the dissemination of early warning information, reinforcing
their leadership in climate adaptation. Collectively, these interventions contribute to inclusiv

economic growth, poverty reduction, and more equitable participation in climate -resilient 
development.  
29. Overall, the sustainable development potential is assessed as high.  
1.5 Needs of the recipient                Scale:  Medium to high  
30. Need for access to finance.  Financial constraints present a major obstacle to climate 
resilience for smallholder farmers in northern Ghana6. Adaptation technologies like drought -
resistant seeds and irrigation systems exist are costly from a farmer’s perspective thus leading 
to low adoption. Agricultural financing could bridge this gap, but access remains severely 
constrained. Local financial institutions (LFIs) typically avoid lending to individual smallholder 
farmers due to four interrelated challenges, i.e., (i) low financial literacy which limits farmers' 
ability to engage with formal banking systems; (ii) absence of credit histories which creates 
uncertainty for risk -averse lenders; (iii) collateral shortages which prevent farmers from 
meeting traditional loan requirements ; and (iv) climate sensitive activities  such as rain -fed 
agriculture increase perceived repayment risks.  This financi al exclusion perpetuates a cycle of 
vulnerability. Output 4 specifically addresses these barriers by developing innovative financing mechanisms tailored to smallholder farmers' unique circumstances, thereby enabling 
investment in critical climate adaptatio n measures. The initiative recognizes that without 
financial solutions that account for these realities, even the most effective adaptation 
technologies will remain out of reach for those who need them most. Project component 2 
output 4 will address this . (See footnote 2).   
31. Urgent need to reduce climate impacts on farmers’ livelihoods in Northern Ghana.  
Climate change is significantly undermining the livelihoods of smallholder farmers in Northern 
Ghana, where most households depend on traditional, rainfed agriculture. Erratic and shorter 
rainy seasons, rising temperatures, and prolonged dry spells have r educed crop productivity, 
increased post -harvest losses, and worsened food insecurity. Limited irrigation options hinder 
dry-season farming, while extreme weather events such a s floods and droughts damage crops, 
livestock, and infrastructure. Degraded ecosystems, frequent bushfires, and dwindling water sources further reduce land productivity. With approximately 37% of Ghana’s labour force in 
agriculture, the declining yields di rectly translate to income instability, food shortages, and 
rising malnutrition. Climate -vulnerable regions like Upper West, Upper East, and North East 
face poverty rates exceeding 70%, reinforcing regional inequalities. Crop failure and livestock 
loss tra p households in cycles of debt and deepen rural poverty, highlighting the urgent need for 
targeted climate adaptation interventions. Project component 2 on climate resilient livelihoods 
will address these.  (See footnote 2)  
32. Need for strengthened CIEWS to reduce vulnerability.  The increasing frequency and 
intensity of climate -related hazards such as droughts, floods, and extreme rainfall in northern 
Ghana highlight the urgent need for CIEWS. Traditional knowledge is no longer sufficient to anticipate these rapidly changing clim ate risks, leaving smallholder farmers and vulnerable 
communities unprepared. Many rural areas lack access to timely, accurate, and locally relevant climate information, limiting their ability to make inf ormed decisions on crop planning, 
livestock management, and disaster response. Strengthening early warning systems by integrating meteorological and hydrological data with community -based dissemination 
channels will enhance preparedness, enable early actio n, and reduce losses. Ensuring that 
warnings are accessible to women, youth, and marginalized groups is critical to building 
inclusive resilience and reducing long -term vulnerability across the region. Project component 1 
will address these.  (See footnote 2)  
 
6 See funding proposal section B.2


---
*Page 104*
---

 
GCF/B. 42/02/Add. 07 
Page 104 
 
 
33. Need for ecosystem -based adaptation (EbA) as a critical response to climate risks.  
The rising frequency and intensity of climate linked hazards (e.g., droughts, floods, and erratic 
rainfall) in Northern Ghana calls for urgent and integrated responses that go beyond 
conventional risk management. Smallholder farmers and vulnerable communi ties struggling 
with limited access to reliable climate information face mounting challenges in anticipating and 
responding to these climate shocks. Strengthening CIEWS is essential, but it must be paired with 
EbA to address the root causes of vulnerability. EbA offers nature -based solutions that restore 
degraded landscapes, enhance water retention, improve soil health, and provide natural buffers 
against climate extremes. EbA  interventions not only mitigate the impacts of hazards but also 
sustain agricultural productivity and livelihoods. Combined with accessible, community -based 
early warning systems especially those inclusive of women, youth, and marginalized groups, 
EbA opt ions becomes a powerful tool for enhancing preparedness, reducing long -term 
vulnerability, and building climate -resilient rural economies. Among the EbA options 
considered are as follows, river bank restoration, agroforestry, reforestation, flood risk 
mana gement and fire risk reduction. Project component 2 output 3 will address this.7 
34. Needs of displaced populations due to impacts of climate. Individuals and 
households affected by flood events typically migrate to urban areas in search of alternate 
livelihoods, usually in non -climate sensitive sectors such as informal trading. Specifically, people 
from the north migrate to the south to stay wit h close relations only for a period and then move 
back. During this period some may change their livelihoods from farming to the service sector 
and then move back. Project components 1 and 2 collect ively address this.  
1.5.1.  Risk to needs of recipients  
35. Gaps in digital finance integration.  Although access to climate finance issues are 
addressed under Project Component 2, Output 4, the potential impact remains constrained by 
the project's limited consideration of digital advancements in agricultural finance.  
36. Overall, the needs of the recipients is assessed as medium to high.  
1.6 Country ownership                  Scale: Medium to high  
37. NOL secured.  UNEP has secured from the National Designated Authority on 8th of April 
2025, signed by the duly authorized representative of the Ministry of Finance, the National 
Designated Authority/ Focal Point of the Republic of Ghana.8 
38. Alignment with Ghana development and climate priorities.  The project is well -
aligned with Ghana’s national climate and development priorities, particularly the National 
Climate Change Policy (NCCP), the National Climate Change Adaptation Strategy (NCCAS), the 
Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs), and the forthcoming National Adaptation Plan 
(NAP). The funding proposal supports key adaptation priorities identified in these frameworks, 
including resilient livelihoods, sustainable agriculture, land and water resource management, 
early warning systems, and dec entralized climate governance. The project contributes directly 
to Ghana’s 2021 NDC adaptation actions by enhancing climate services, strengthening early 
warning and disaster risk management systems, and promoting climate -resilient livelihoods for 
women an d youth in vulnerable agro -ecological zones. These priorities are expected to remain 
central in the upcoming NDC 3.0 (2025). The project complements nature- based adaptation 
measures under the agriculture sector outlined in the draft NAP, being finalized un der the GCF 
Readiness programme led by the Environment Protection Agency (EPA) and UNEP. The project 
reflects strong government ownership and coherence with national planning processes that

have been developed through extensive consultations across national, regional, district, and 
community levels.9 
39. Demonstrated track record and core expertise of the implementing partners in 
Ghana. As Executing Entity, the EPA will implement the project in partnership with the Ministry 
of Food and Agriculture ( MoFA ) and the GMet  both of which have established records in 
delivering large -scale climate and environmental programs in their respective fields of 
expertise. The EPA, under the Ministry of Environment, Science, Technology and Innovation 
(MESTI), serves as the national foc al point for the UNFCCC and has led land mark climate and 
environmental initiatives, including the Ghana Environmental Management Project (GEMP), the 
Sustainable Land and Water Management Project (SLWMP), and the GCF -financed National 
Adaptation Plan (NAP). EPA brings expertise in land restoratio n, climate -resilient agriculture, 
and EbA, and will coordinate implementation in collaboration with MoFA , the GMet , and the 
Water Resources Commission (WRC). MoFA  will provide technical support to FBOs, promote 
access to finance, and train extension officers in climate -smart agriculture. GMet , with its 
technical expertise in climate forecasting and drought monitoring, will lead improvements in early warning systems under Output 1, while WRC will integrate hydrological data to 
strengthen climate risk assessments. These agencies have established  effective coordination 
mechanisms and governance structures through prior multi -sectoral projects, ensuring their 
readiness to deliver targeted interventions and achieve project outcomes at scale. MESTI will 
chair the Project Steering Committee (PSC), ensuring strategic oversight and alignment with 
national climate and development priorities.

1.6.1.  Risk to county ownership 
40. Complex implementation.  The implementation arrangements between and amongst 
the Executing Entities and Implementing Entities show a complex coordination set up which 
risks potential delays, miscommunication, inefficiencies, and ineffective execution of project 
activities and ach ievement of project deliverables and outcomes. Absent copies of the 
Memorandum of Understanding (MoU), or agreements, iTAP raised these issues with the AE 
that the MoU appears as a “best efforts” arrangement compared to the “agreemen ts” (i.e., 
Project Cooperation Agreement, grant agreements/ contracts)11 which are likely to be more 
solid legal documentations.  In response, the AE stated that “ As part of updating the FP (Figures 
5 and 6 and associated text) and the term Sheet (Annex 14), and following consultations with 
EPA Head of Legal Department, and whilst MOUs can be stated as legally binding, MOUs will be 
replaced with legal binding Implem entation Agreements between EPA and GMET , EPA and 
MOFA , and GMET  and WRC.  The legally binding Implementation Agreements will specify roles, 
responsibilities, timelines, deliverables and financial commitments.”12 
41. Limited Capacity of EPA to Implement Output 4 and Recommended Measures. 
While the EPA has strong credentials in environmental governance and climate policy 
implementation, its has limited expertise in financial systems, and this presents a significant 
risk to the effective delivery of Output 4: “Increased access of smallholder  farmers to financial 
resources and engagement with the private sector.” EPA lacks institutional experience in key 
areas such as financial product design, blended finance structuring, credit risk assessment, and 
financial literacy programming. Specific gaps include developing credit scoring systems and 
actuarial models, supporting Business Advisory Centres (BACs) and LFIs, and facilitating the 
establishment and regulation of FBOs and VS LAs. These capacity constraints may compromise 
the technical soundness and financial viability of interventions and limit EPA’s ability to

coordinate complex, multi -stakeholder engagement across rural areas where institutional

may affect the creation and management of secure, reliable databases need ed for credit

FBOs, promote access to finance, and train extension officers in climate -smart agriculture;
